Mr. Collier then moved that the stun of £750 be p•id to the Directors of the Company as an honorarium for their services which was se:ionded by Mr. T. D, Timmins, but the Chairman ruled that as the notice convening the meeting would hardly warrant a resolution to that effect being passed he could not put it to the meeting ; whereupon Mr. Collier moved that, an Extraoroinary General Meeting of the Shareholders of the Company he convened to be held in the Company's Offices, the Public Hall, Runcorn, en MoN-- DAY the 28th day of MAY 1894, at 3 o'clock in the afternoon, when he would move that the sum of £750 be paid to the Directors of the Com panyas an honorarium for their services. "CAPITAL" CHAT.

There are already fourteen pages of amendments to the Registration Bill, but this is probably only a tithe of what we may expect. One Conservative member has drafted between thirty and forty amendments, not one of which yet appears on the paper. In point of fact, the Opposition amend- ments 80 far mostly stand in the names of Liberal Unionists. In regard to the plural voting provisions, I understand that the Cocservative leaders will aim at their complete excision.

With regard to the South Hackney election,l may add that a good deal of criticism has been excited in London Liberal circles by the methods adopted by Conservative candidates V. ho are assiduously " nursing " London constituencies. The device of free concerts and the like is carried to an extreme which, in the opinion of many, is in conflict with the Corrupt rract ices Act. The subject is a burning one with an influential section of the ?louse of Commons, and I understand that it is quite likely that a test case may be brought before the courts.

Canon Browne has just made a remarkable state- ment in regard to the revenues which the Ecclesias- tical Commission derives from St. Paul's Cathedral. It would appear that they amount to from ..£150,000 to £160,000 every year, or nearly seven timPs as much as the sum paid to the whole of the Cathedral staff. The ecclesiastical revenues from the whole of London are said to be enormohs ; and it is a sore point with many Churchmen that although certain grants are made to lixidon,the bulk of the money is distributed over the country, leaving Londoners to raise by voluntary effort much of what is required for Church extension.

Some extraordinary rumours are afloat respecting the new relations between the Parnellites and the Tory party. One story is that in future the two parties will work together in perfect accord, and that an assurance has been ;en that the Tories may rely upon the Parnellites for support in all critical divisions. Ido not deem it prudent to state the conditions on which it is openly stated that such an understanding has been arrived at. I prefer to hope, for the sake of the Home Rule cause, that the extraordinary report at present being circulated may be without foundation.

The Queen looked very pale on Wednesday when she arrived at Buckingham Palace. She seemed to feel the cold wind, for in spite of the fact that there was no rain or sun her Majesty kept herself shel- tered by a black parasol. This caused 901118 grumbl- ing auiong,st the people who had waited to catch a fieeting glimpse of her Majesty and her convoy of Life Guards and semi-State carriages. Princess Alix of Hesse, the betrothed of the Cesarewich, occupied a seat opposite her Majesty. She is really a beau- tiful girl, fit to be the future Czar of Russia's wife. Thg wedding is to take place in October at St. Petersburg. 1 hear that there is no truth in the report that the Queen will be present.

The Church Missionary Society is about to extend its work among the Esquiraaux. The Rev. E. J. Peck, who laboured for many years in the diocese o f Moosonee under the late Bishop llorden, is. now in England, and he will shortly leave for Cumber- land sound and push his way further north into the _Arctic regions. Mr. Peek, who will be accom- panied by a layman, will sail from the extreme north of Scotland ; and the proprietor of a large number of whaling vessels has generously promised to give him and his compani-inn a free passage out and back. The enterprise will be watched with interest, for Mr. Peck purposes to push on some hundreds of miles beyond the furthest northern utpost of the Church „Missionary Society.

We may be fully prepared now for a deliberate attempt on the part of th? Opposition after Whit- suntide to force a crisis. I hear that a leading official of the Conservative party used the follow- ing words yesterday, which may be quoted literally because they probably represent very exactly the dominant feeling among the shrewder men on the Oppo-ition side. " I don't see," he said, " what either sid.. has to gain by a general election at the present time. But I i!olft see at the same time. how we can afford to let the Government go on." One of rhe nros!-. active spirits in what is known as the " stalwart sezition above the Oppo-ition gang- way has in the last day or two given expression to a very similar idea. Tie outlook may not be very promising for the Unionists, but to mi3s their chances at the present time would, he hol,:s, 118 far as the Opposition is concerned, deliberately playing into the hands oi the Government. There is no evidecce that Tie Liberal prospects will grow worse, but, on the contrary, there is every fair pre- tumption that if the Opposition allow the Govern- ment to go on quietly developi-; g their programme, the Ministerial chancs will become better, so that it looks as it the Opposition generally would be driven by the force of circurn,tances and in spite of •themselves into the forward course advocaLed by Mr. Chamberlain.

The re-appnram e of Mr. Gladstone for the first time since his withdrawal from public life on Thurs- day last week, 'la! urally caused n good deal of sym- pathetic gos,ip with which has been mixed up much -inaccurate rurr. our. It may ba sufficient to say here that Mr. Gladstone's weakness and his inability to stand by no means represented his normal con- dition, but were due to temporary debility arising from a passing ailment of which he had had a troublesome recurrence, and from which he is not yet quite fre-,. On favourable days Mr. Gladstone moves about qlite freely, and Ntith the erect and spriztlt ly gait which is characteristic of hitt. He is still able to read in a good light. Mr. Giad.-7 stone does not enjoy being read to, and the tempor- Ivy improvement in sight effected by the artificial •dilatation of tne iris is a great solace to him. It is quite untrue that an date has yet. been fixed for the operation, nor is it likely that it will be so fixed until the complete ripening of the cataracts is effected naturally, and a more vigorous state of health established.

The cif cumstances under which the existence of Cataract in Mr. Gladstone's eyes was first discovered are pathetic and in a sense dramatic. It will be re- membered that whilst Mr. Gladstone was driving to a meeting to make a speecn at Chester, a missile thrown by a woman struck him in the eye, and he was obliged to have the eye dres,ed and to take advice as to whether it would • be safe to make a public ap;eartince. The injured eye had to he closed and protected from the light and air. It was then discovered that the other eye had very little power of sight, and on closer examination it was f Rind to - be the seat of an already well-formed cataract. The powerof sight has thus been lost in the one eye for nearly two years, and it is the formation of a se- cond cataract in the injured eye which which has withdrawn Mr. Gladstone for the present from public life and which will shortly necessitate an operation. Happily this operation t now success- ful in about 9S per cent. of the cases at whatever age.

.

AN EIGHT-HOURS DAY DEMANDED. LONDON.

A gigantic demonstration of workingmon was held on Sunday afternoon in Hyde Park, London, in favour of a legal eight-hours day. During the forenoon contin- gents from all parts of the metropolis were assembling on the Thames embankment.and an imposing procession to the park by way of Northumberland avenue and Pic- cadilly was witnessed by many thousands of the general public. There was less congestion of traffic than has been occasioned at some previous demonstrations of the kind, and this was due alike to the tact of the marshals and the judgment and discretion of the force of mounted police within the park. There were twelve platforms, from which labour leaders delivered addresses; five stations were allotted to the Legal Eight Hours League, and there were present at the international platform, amon4st others, M. Jules Guesde,a member of the French Chamber. A resolution in favour of an international eight-hour day was adopted at's!! platforms.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
LEEDS.

A labour demonstration in Leeds took place on Sun- day on Woodhouse Moor, under the auspices of the In- dependent Labour Party. Prior to assembling on the Moor the members of the various labour clubs and socie- ties paraded the principal streets of the city. Speeches were made from two platforms which had been erected. The following resolution was adopted, amid loud cheer- ing :—" That this meeting reaffirms the principle of an eight-hours working day, and urges the+ election of Independent labour representatives on all governin g bodies to enforce it."

===== 0002601_18940512_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTTINGHAM.

One of the largest labour demonstrations witnessed in Nottingham for many years took place on Saturday, when over 20,000 male and female operatives, represent- ing various trades union organizations, marched in pro- cession to the Forest, the chief open space in the town, where a mass meeting was held. Speeches were de- livered from three platforms, and resolutions were simultaneously passed condemning the action of the House of Lords in reference to the Employers' Liability Bill, and urging the Government to reintroduce the measure; also calling upon them to promote a Bill to regulate the hours of labour, eight daily, in all trades, and prohibiting employment of children under 14.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
NO.RTFIAMP roN.

Labour day in Northampton aroused little enthusiasm. After parading the streets a meeting was held, and reso- lutions were passed from two platforms in favour of an eight hours day and the nationalization of the means of production. One precession was under the management of the Northampton Trades Council, and the speakers included Councillor D. Stanton. J.P. The other was composed of local Socialists, and the chief speaker was Comrade Jones, a London Socialist candidate for the borough.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
EDINBURGH.

On Saturday afternoon there was a labour demonstra- tion in Edinburgh organized by the Trades Council of the Independent Labour Party. The procession through the city comprised about 5,000 members of the labour or- ganizations. Resolutions were passed demanding an eight hours day and the democratic control of industry.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
GLASGOW.

In Glasgow the weather was inclement, and less than 10,000 persons attended. The Anarchists had a separate platform. They asserted that no good could be got from the ballot box or by vDting for Mr. Keir Hardie or Mr. John Burns. One Anarchist said be would rather vote for Lord Randolph Churchill. The Labour party had four platforms, at which resolutions were passed de- manding the abolition of the House of Lords with no second chamber in its stead, also that an Eight Hours Day Bill be decreed by Parliament, and that land and industrial capital be nationalized.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
DUNDEE.

At Dundee on Saturday resolutions in favour of a legal eight hours Oay were carried. Mr. Keir Flardie criticized the Prime Minister's speech at Manchester. He denied Lord Rosebery's assertirin that the Labour party drew their membership exclusively from the Liberal party, and declared that wherever Labour candidates had gone to the poll it had been proved that one-third, and in some cases nearly half the votes cast for the Labour candidates came from workingtnen who lomerly sup- ported the Conservatives. The Liberals won the last election through the support of the L Amur party, but they would not again play into the hands of an effete and worn-out Liberalism. DUBLIN.

About 10,000 persons attended the labour demonstra- tion in Phoenix Park, D4hlin, on Sunday. The usual procession through the city took place. The people were addressed from three plattorms erected in the park and the speakers included Dr. Kenny, M.P., and Mr. Field, M.P. A series of resolutions were put from elch Vatfcrm and adopted callinz for an international reduc- tion ot the hours of labour to eight per day, manhood suffrage, assimilations of the boronA parliamen!ary franchise, labour representation in T'Arliament and. on local boards, and the State payment of members of Parliament.

Labour demonstrations were also held at Burnley and Bristol, and in each case an eight heurs labour day was demanded. At Briitol the demonstrators marched through the streets ~cith bands and banners.

BRUTAL OFFENCE AT WIDNES. GRUESOME PRODUCTION IN COURT.

JUSTICE DAY ON JUSTICE TO MAD DOGS.

Before Mr. Justice Day, at the Liverpool Assizes, on Friday, Elizabeth Bushell, aged thirty-seven, of no occupation, was indicted for having wounded Margaret M`Cann, at Widnes, on the 2nd April. Mr. Formby prosecuted, and Mr. Segar appeared for the defence. The prosecutrix said that the prisoner came to her house and attacked her. She VS";ts knocked down, and the prisoner attempted to bite her nose off. She prevented her doing so, and the Prisoner !hen bit a piece out of her ear. On being cross-examined, the prosecutrix said she had been before the courts four times. The prisoner and herself fought. They had hold of each other by the hair.—Mr. Segar: You are rather good at fighting.—Prosecutrix : Yes, I can take my own part if she had let my ear alone without hiting.—A wit- ness said that the prosecutrix wanted to go on with the fight when the prisoner Went home. Both the prisoner and prosecutrix were worrying each other like dogs. The piece of the prosecutrix's ear which had been bitten off was produced in a bottle in court.— Mr. Segar, for the defence, submitted that what th:,, prisoner did was done in self defence. The prosecutrix and the prisoner were on the ground worrying each other and fighting Ike mad dogs.—Flis Lordship : In justice to mad dogs, Mr. Segar, you ought not to say that. A mad dog snaps and goes on, and does not stop biting. (Laughter,)—The jury, after considerable consultation, fowl(' the prisoner guilty of unlawful wounding.—A Police-constable from Widnes said the prisoner had been twentc-rine times convicted, and had suffered twelve months' imprisonment for wounding with intent to do grievous bodily harm.—His Lordship said the prisoner had commi,ted a brutal offence. She must be imprisoned and k=lit at hard labour for twenty-one calendar months.

Ellen Hunt, 40, charwoman, was found guilty for hav- ing at Widnes on the 14th April, 1894, unlawfully wounded William Shannon, by throwing a saucer. It was stated that the prisoner had been 30 times previ- ously convicted, and had served one term of seven years' penal servitude. His Lordship nnw sentenced her to five years' penal servitude. The prisoner on hearing the sentence turned to the prosecutor and his witnesses and exclaimed, " May my curse go with you. You'll not have much luck when I come back."

AN ATHLETE'S DIET.

" What is the best dietary on which an athlete should train ?" is a quastion often put. Sir Benjamin IVard Richardson has four rpecifics :—Nhstinencm from hurtful

thin eg ; regular and good habits ; calmnes< of temp-r; laudable ambition. He demands, along with all good trainers and all good competitlrs, as absolutely neces- sary, abstinence from alcohol. He also forbids tQbacco smoking. Gambling he regards as fatal to body and mind. Among other things commended by the well- known physician is the following table of dietary :—Oat- meal porridge and eggs with toast make a good break- fast ; a mutton chop or a beef steak, with a light quan- tity of vegetables and some fruit, make an efficient din- ner • and avoiding tea, or exchanging that for a cup ot milkt,a, dish of oatmeal porridge for supper suffices. These, in his opinion, form as good a diet role as may he devised tor men in active athletic work. Then the further advises three or four meals a day, three or four hours apart; seven hours' sleep, and " early to bed and early to rise."

The election of a member of Parliament for South Hackney in the place of Sir Charles Russ. 11, elevated to the office of Lord of Appeal, tor.l; place on Monday. The candidate R were Mr. F. Moulton (L) and Mr. T. H. Robertson (C). The result was announced at a late hour, and was as follows .—Moulton. 4,530 : Robertson, 4,338.; Liberal majority, 192. Th- fizures in 1886 were :—Russell (L), 2,800 ; Darline (C), 2.700 Liberal majority, 100 : ia 1892 Russell (L) 4,440, Robertson (C), 3,294—Liberal majority, 1,146.

THE EXAMINER, SATUR PAY, MAY 12, 1894.

Marion Crawford is said to receive two thousand pounds tor each of his novels, lie always writes two during a year. The Queen of Greece is president of a sisterhood devoted to the reformation of criminals, aud she person- ally visits prisoners. The most noted shot among English women is lady Eva Quin, wife of Captain Wyndham, heir-presumptive to the Earl of Dunraven. She has killed six full-grown tigers from the frail shelter of A howdah. Miss Ada Crossley, who has just arrived from Austra- lia. is admitted by all who are qualined to judge, to be a most highly gifted contralto, says "Spare Moments." She comes from Melbourne, and already she has ad- mirers who declare that her fame will some day equal that of Madame Melba, who hails from the same city. During the past two years her singing has attracted much attention, and aroused great enthusiasm in Aus- tralia. Miss Crossley is twenty-three years of age, and is extremely pretty. The lady who was recently elected Mayor of Pleasan- town, in Kansas, has dismissed all the police. The mem- bers of the force were superseded by zealous Prohibi- tionists, who immediately closed all the public-houses and gambling saloons. The Lady Mayoress has also commanded that all restaurants shall be closed by ten p.m. Furthermore, no boy or girl under sixteen may be out of doors after nine without special permission. But this is not all. The new governess proposes the immediate dismissal of all the members of the Municipal Council if they will not give in to her views on public morality and general economy. Pleasant, place, Pleasantown.

The ladies are still continuing to break new ground for themselves. Lady Mildred Jessup,a younger daugh- ter of Lord and Lady Strathmore,is the latest wonder of the world. She is the first woman on record who has written a successful opera. The measure which Lady Mildred has secured may be gauged when it is borne in mind that the work was presented anonymously to the fastidious musical public of Florence and that on the very first night it excitKl enthusiastic approval. The name of the composer was not dischsed until the se- cond night, when LAdy Mildred, who is only a little more than twenty-five years of age, and has been mar- ried four years, came before the curtain to bow her ac- knowledgements.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
L ADIES' LETTER.

Ikf I'II4IZ EST NELLIE,

J ickets are certainly shorter than last year, the betques remaining moderately full, and tight coats are more in vogue than loose ones, although the open-fronted style is still worn with skirts to match. Moire sleeves are seen on the majority of cloth and serge jackets, and many a last year's coat has been brought up to date and made to look quite fresh and new by adding fresh sleeves and a ~ng ended neck-bow of the all-prevailing fabric. Many of the open•fronted jackete turn back with broad revers, but fasten at the waist, or can be worn completely open. The plain coloured vests of decided colouring have not entirely eclipsed the checks and stripes in " horsecloth" designs, but the self-celoured blues, ranging from a smoke- colour with bluish tinge to a deep Nile blue, and including the popular "butcher' tint are run very close by a brilliant reddish tan, and a dull rose which is yet not at all pink. The new jacket fits closely to the figure, and has full skirts at the back, while the fronts are fitted with long darts, from which a small belt keeps the front edges to- -.ber. The bquare-altaped collar hangs in sailor fashion aerpris the back of the shoulders, while at the same time the ordinary collar Rnd revers could be eubetitw el if desired.

Je.ckets, however, do not monopol;se our favour; apes are equally fashionable, and a very pretty one I have seen is made of moire Frangaise. Both capes are very full, for such is the fashionable order of the day, and both are embroidered with jet reguine. There are long stole ends in front, the lace being that known as Bourdon. As long as the large sleeves remain in vogue capes will be worn, as it is utterly impossible to force a jacket over such a mass of material as the immense gigot sleeves represent, therefore we can count 'upon the capes for the spring and summer. Moire, satin duchesse, and broadcloth are a comparatively limited range of materials, buo the colourings and trimmings make the variety. The silken materials may be said to be universally of black, but in the cloth shades of browo, golden-tan, navy-blue, dark green, and grey will be worn. The elaborate capes will make a feature of handsome linings in moire, satin, brocade, and changeable taffeta ; the cloth capes are seldom lined, even though much trimmed. Cream and black Bourdon and guipure laces and jet will trim the silken wraps, with lace, silk, or braid for those of cloth.

A useful and booming hat for present wear is made of fine brown straw, lined with velvet, and daintily trimmed with bows and ends of brown satin, intermixed with jetted osprey and yellow mimosa blossom, while a delicate spray of the same fl-rwcr is arranged round the foot of the crown. Very original is a hat of green and gold fancy straw, trimmed with a bow of canary-coloured ribbon anti two large green chrysanthemums. A stylish hat is of fine black straw, trimmed with turquoise-blue velvet and three paste buckles placed at intervals, and a cluster of pink roses at the side. A dainty creation ha z a wreath of rose- piok roses running round under the brim, and tailing upon the hair in becoming fashion. A stylish-looking toque I have also seen is of black lace. Cold Feet.

What a common complaint this is! and yet no one seems to know anything about it. You suffer from it for years, and yet you do not go to a doctor, or if you de,you derive very little benefit from his advice. Some people suffer from it at night only, whilst others are troubled in the daytime as well. It. occurs most fre- quently in women, but still you often hear men complain of it. We believe that the best remedy is hypophosphite of lime in one or two grain doses twice a Clay. This is soluble in water, and should be taken in the form of a mixture, nothing else being put with it, with the excep- tion, if you like, of a teaspoonful of syrup, to make it more patatable, although it is really by no means dis- agreeable by itself. Another good remedy is nux vomica —five drops of the tincture in a little water three or four times a day. It is highly recommended, and you may hope for great things from it. Then you must do all you possibly can to improve the state of yAur general health. It is probable that you are below par somehow or other, although you must admit that it does not fol- low of necessity. If you feel generally out of sorts, and I your appetite is poor, quinine will do Toll good. If you are pale and antemic, you must put your faith in iron. Parrish's Chemical Food often does good. Cod-liver oil is an excellent remedy for improving the general nutri- tion; many people feel quite in a glow after each dose. You should live as well as possible, and a glass or two of port wine a day will do you good. Cold bathing in the morning will quicken the eirculation for you. good brisk walk, if you are able to take it, soon warms the feet. It is a great thing to be properly shod; good stout, well-fitting boots, with thick, warm socks. If you for any reason are unable to get walking exercise, you will -find that five or ten minutes' exercise with the dumb-bells in your room before breakfast is not a thing to be despised. The hot-water bottle in bed at night is a palliative, but does nothin,, to effect a cure. Many people who have suffered from cold feet have ass ured us that they have derived the greatest benefit from putting; them into cold water at bed time. It seems a disagree- able remedy, but they say that the reaction which immediately follows the primary chili is &lig htitil, and that the plan succeeds admirably. The best way would be to rub the fer.t quite dry with a bath-ti wel after bathing them, and then to jump into a warm bed.— "The Family Physician."

How to Produce Ozone.

Everyone knows the sick-headachy sensation and depression of spirits which is felt in a close, airless, stifl- ing atmosphere such as that of a crowded theatre or concert-room, or a badly-ventilated room ; and on the contrary, the feeling of physical comfort and well-being that accompanies a walk through a garden of sweet- smelling flowers or a lane bordered with hedges of sweet briar and honeysuckle, or by the sea-shore, or in a pine wood. In the latter cases tile air is highly charged with fragrant ( z me, every breath of which renews and purifies our blood ; in the former, the ozone has been exhausted, breathed up, as it were, by the assembled multitude Of human lungs, arid its place taken by the noxious and poisonous carbonic acid gas breathed nut again by the same lungs, and which is, I need hardly point out, hurtful in the extreme to the system and the general health,and absolutely ruinous to the complexion. A simple and easy method of rapidly generating oz.me to supply the place of that exhausted from the atmos- phere of a small, badly-ventilated, or over-crowded room (if the best ozone-producers, fresh air and sunshine, cannot be obtained in sufficient quantity), is to sprinkle some oil of vitriol, diluted, over some perniviganate of potash, but, of course, great care mus', be taken to put it in a place where no children can get at it, and where it will not be likely to be upset, as oil of vitrol is a very powerful and corroding acid.

How to use Perfumes.

Haring selected your favourite perfume, the best and most delicate way of identifying it with yourself is not t. saturate your handkerchiefs or your underlinen with sudden dashes of the scent from a b ittle, but to keep amongst your underlinen 'little powder sachets scented with the perfume, or, which is also a good plan, to make for each one of your linen drawers a pad of cotton-wool covered with art muslin or silk, and exactly fitting the bottom of the drawer ; scent this well from the bottle, renewing the process from time to time,and on it spr,wi your underlinen, handkerchiefs, &c., &c., which will acquire and retain a delicate perfume. never decid•A enough to be pronounced and " voyante," but just enough to be fresh,sweet. and delicately fragrant. Your dresses you can scent in the same fashion either by sew- ing in little scented pads,or if you keep them in a ward- robe. puttin% a similar pad of covered cottonwool on the floor of the wardrobe, or hanging it as a. back zround to the dresses, and scenting it strongly in the same way , as those of the linen drawers. In a very short time you will find that all your things will have a delicate fr.i- ; grance pervading them,and even your bedroom or dress- I img room will be fresh and sweet with A faint delicate suggestion of the same perfume ever lingering in the air.

A 'IIATIEN7II PEOPLE.

-- There are several penples whose patience under mis- rule, suffering, starvation, and other woes has been and is remarkable, but in its way the patience of the E=ltimo is singular. Knowing nothing of the high pressure under which English-speaking folk live nowadays, there is, says " Little Folks," of course, no reason why they should not take things most easily, and this they seem to do very well. It is related of a number of them that they were sent one summer to cut the grass of a meadow at some distance from their homes. They were gone a long time, but when they came back they explained they had had to pitch their tents and wait until the grass was long enough to mow. "WELL PLAYED, HOMOCEA."

Ey the Author of " DAUKWOOD." .

CHAPTER XII. ON PERILOUS GROUND., I stand as one upon a rock,

Environ'd with a wilderne-ss of sea ; Who marks the waxing tide grow, wave by wave, Expecting ever when some envious surge Will in his brinish bowels swallow him. - SHAKESPEARE.

Giralds, was conducted to the housekeeper's room, where, for the next hour, she was left to solitude and her own thoughts. At the end of the period mentioned Mrs. Pumpton returned, breath- ing heavily as from over-exertion, and announced that the young lady's chamber was in readiness. " Your travelling bag has been carried up, Miss," she said. "Have you no other luggage ? " "No:, hcre," replied the young girl, rising. " I shall have more soon, as I intend making some purchases at the village." The maiden's manner was reticent, and the housekeeper forebore to press her inquiries. She therefore led the way in silence to Giralda's apart- ments.

They entered the wide ball of the main edifice. It had a strangely dismantled look, being totally bare of furniture. They ascended the grand stair- case, their steps echoing hollowly, and gained the upper hall, which was but a counterpart of the lower.

"My lord has had the carpets taken up and the furniture put away, there being no company now- a-days at the Park," said the housekeeper, apolo- getically, as she conducted the maiden to a cham- ber opening off the hall. "This is your room, Miss."

She flung open i door near the head of the stairs, and ushered Giro.lda Into an apartment, herself following. The room was wide and high, almost square in shape. It had a wide window looking to the east and a magnificent oriel fronting the south, so that all the light and sunshine of the outer world seemed to concentrate here. The walls were adorned with pictures. A wood fire burned and sparkled in the ample old-fashioned fireplace, giving to the air a delightful temperature. In an alcove stood a high, four post bed-stead, heaped with lace-frilled pillows. A dressing-table, draped with pink muslin and white lace, its mirror framed in a cloud of rosy drapery, stood in one cor- ner. A Turkish lounge and an easy chair com- pTised the luxuries of the apartment, but they were old and threadbare, as was also the .Turkey carpet, whose bright hue even time and wear had not been able to efface.

" What a pleasant room !" exclaimed Giralda, advancing to the oriel window and looking across the lawn to the park, where the trees were waving like billows in the restless March wind.

The housekeeper's worn face brightened. "I am glad you like it, Miss," she answered. "I suppose my lord max be diFuleaed when he finds what room I have given you, but Lord Ad- lowa has the blue chamber, and his valet had to have the next chamber, instead of lodging among the servants, where he belongs, and the rest of the rooms are stripped of furniture. Ny lord had the carpets and furniture boxed up years ago. This room he never had touched and he hasn't entered it for nearly eighteen years." "Doesn't he like it? " asked Giralda, wonder- _ _

The housekeeper hesitated-. Something in the young girl's appearance seemed to invite her con- fidence.

" It—it was Mr. Geoffrey's room," she whis pered.

Giralda shivered.

" Mr. Geoffrey's'!" she repeated. 44 The land- lord at the "Trevelyans Arms" told me about him. lie tried to murder and rob his uncle."

The housekeeper's face flushed, and her eyes sparkled with sudden feeling. " Tie was caught in his uncle's room, miss, at midnight, a knife in his hand. Eis confusion and bewilderment, added to his situation, seemed to fix the intention of an awful crime upon him ! But I know he never meant to harm his uncle. Lord Trevelya,n was harsh with him, and kept !inn upon short allowances of money, hut Geof- frey Trevelyan was incapable of harming a hair of the old man's head. There is some mystery about that night's affair, and when that mystery is unraveled, Geoffrey's name will be cleared. He was the noblest, the brightest, the gayest of boys, Miss Arevalo. He had a kind word and a smile for every one. The village children laughed and crowed when he came near. The boys followed at his horse's heels. The girls blushed when handsome Mr. Geoffrey's blue eyes chanced to fall upon them, and the old folks came to him with all their troubles. He nursed his uncle through a contagious fever, when every one else forsook him, and he saved my son's life from drowning. That was the man, Miss Arevalo, whom they accuse of robbery and attempted murder."

" Was there any doubt of his guilt ?" asked Giralda.

" No one doubted it but me," said Mrs. Pump- ton, sorrowfully. " I was his old nurse, and I knew him better than the rest. I knew him to lie incapable of crime. The servants and villagers doubt his guilt now. The poor boy was betrothed to a great lady, an earl's daughter, the Lady Bea- trice Hampton. But they all turned from him when my lord did. And my boy, my pretty nursling, my noble young master, fled off to the Brazils, ant died there ! So it is said, Miss Arevola. It is well that he is thought dead, for my lord would hunt the earth over for him if he believed he lived. My lord loved the boy in his inmost heart with snore than a father's love. Master Geoffrey's suppoeed ingratitude seems to have turned that love to hatred. No one but me knows how my lord worshipped his gay and hand- some heir. But now he would kill him with his Own hands, if he were to meet him !" •

"You speak as if his death were not proven," remarked Giralda.

The housekeeper bestowed upon the maiden a swift, keen glance. Her honest face was full of emotion. Her manner was full of secrecy and mystery. Having read Giralda's pure and noble soul in her face, she glanced apprehensively at the door, and said in a low tone: "Miss Arevalo, since the day Master Geoffrey went away, my lord has shut himself up, seeing no visitors excepting his nephew, Lord Adlowe. Once in a while he goes up to London, but not often. He will not let me speak favourably of my young master, and no one else cares to urge Mas- ter Geoffrey's innocence. I suppose no one but me really believes in it. Your coming here is a remarkable event. Perhaps your innocent ways may soften my lord's heart. Perhaps your blue eyes—so like Master Geoffrey's—may touch his hardened soul ! I don't know why I speak so freely to you, miss, but I beg of you now to u.,,e your influence in behalf of my young master! Lord Trevelyan has taken a fancy to you already, I can see. For the sake of right and justice I en- treat you to speak a good word now and then for Master Geoffrey. He will hearken to you sooner than to me, or I am old and humble, and you are young, beautiful, and a lady. Besides, you have Master Geoffrey's eyes, and my lord loved those gay and innocent eyes ! " The housekeeper spoke entreatingly, her manner full of suppressed eagerness. "I fear I can do nothing to help you, Mrs. Pumpton," said Giralda, with a sigh. "Is it well to stir up these old things? I feel interested in poor Mr. Geoffeey, and I shall like to believe him innocent, whatever the proofs against him. But he is dead, Mrs. Pumpton. Why not let the story of his past die, too ? ' The housekeeper again glanced apprehensively towards the door.

"They said Master Geoffrey died," she Whis- pered. " They sent home the proofs. I believed the story, miss, anti many a night I've cried all night, thinking of a lonely grave off in South America. My son is a sailor, miss, and last year he went to Rio, a big place down there in Brazil. I charged him to find Master Geoffrey's grave, and to put up a marble headstone. I sent the money for the stone out of my own earnings. My son looked, but he could not find the grave. He applied to the Spaniard at whose house Mastcr Geoffrey was said to have died, and he found out, after promising not to injure the fellow, that the ,Aory of Master Geoffrey's death was not true— that he did not die in Brazil." Giralda uttered an exclamation of surprise.

" My fou discovered," continued ihe house- keeper, her aitation increasing, " that Master i;colfrey left Rio soon after for the West Indies. My young master may be living still, Miss Are- vale. Sometimes I even fancy he may be in Eng- land. If my lord should discover that he is not dead, he would set a detective at once upon his :Ci, 4/1d not sci;;;;I: unt:l h hu lita sent to Penal

servitude. Di ow, --win you not help me to soften my lord '3 In_yrt towards his nephew ? if in piling master should live—should be in En,dand--" , ' -

Her voi:.:e failed her. She could only look her agonised pleading. " I will try to help you, Mrs. Pompton," sal,' Giralda, with gentle kin.;liness. "If I were only sure that the young man was guiltless of the crime with which he was charged ! But he could not be innocent. How came he in his unele's chamber at miiinight, a knife in his hand, unless he meant to kill him ? The landlord of the " Tre- velya.n Arms "told me that 'Mr. Geollrey actually stabbed his uncle in the shin lder.. I cannot plead for an intended murderer, except to beg that Lord Trevelyan will forgive the wrong as lie hopes to be forgiven his errors." The housekeeper groaned, and twisted her hands nervously together. Suddenly an idea occurred to her. She sprang up and moved towards a picture, which had already attracted Girabia's attention from the fact that its face was turned to the wall.

Reversing the picture, Mrs. Plimpton exclaimed : " Theren is Master Geoffrey's portrait, painted only a short time before be left home. Look at it, Miss Arevalo, and tell me if you think he could have been guilty of attempted murder." Giralda approached the picture and looked at it steadily. It was that of a youth of one-and twenty, with a fair, boyish face, fair hair of a rare golden tint, a frank and pleasant mouth, anti dark, sunny blue eyes, full of the brightness of a joyous soul. They were truthful, honest eyes, radiant and resolute, yet with a strange tenderness lying in their blue depths. Those eyes had a strange fascination for Gina.la. She did not know how iike her own eyes were to them. It seemed to her that her father was look- ing at her from behind a blonde mask. "Do you think the owner of that face could have committed, or designed to commit, murder?" demanded the housekeeper. " No, no !" cried Giraltia, almost involuntarily. " I should as soon suspect my own father of a crime My father's eyes are very like those, only graver and more sorrowful ; but papa is dark, and his hair is black. He is a paniard. Mr. Geoffrey has papa's eyes, and I will do my best, Mrs. Pump- ton, to influence Lord Trevelyan in his nephew's favour. When I look at the picture it ahnosu seems to me that I shall be working for papa—my own darling papa " she added, softly, under her breath.

The housekeeper failed to catch the last sen- tence, but she seemed overjoyed at the maiden's promise, and was profuse in her thanks. "It may seem strange to you, miss, that I should ask you, a perfect stranger, to take this interest in our family affairs, but I can get no one to speak a word for my poor young master. And every day I dread lest he should have come back to England and he discovered. Do not tell my lord that his nephew possibly lives, Miss Arevalo. Wait until he shows some sign of softening to- wards Mae ' Geoffrey. He is an awful man in his rages—awful ! " and Mrs. Pumpton shud- dered. "If you can only make him think differ- ently of poor Mr. Geoffrey ! Every one has tricd to inflame him against him—every one but me ! She lingered to give a few more injunctions to the young girl, whose promised aid filled her faithful heart with hopes, and then withdrew, -having promised to send up the maiden's luncheon at the proper hour. Left alone, Giralda, looked long and earnestly at the portrait of Geoffrey Trevelyan, which smiled down upon her with tender, familiar eyes. " I will work for Mr.. Geoffrey," she said, aloud, her face kindling with resolution. ''lt will al- most seem to me that I am working for papa," she added, unconsciously repeating what she had said before. "I will try to fancy that lam doing it for papa's sake ;" and she smiled faintly at the very absurdity of the idea. Turning reluctantly from the portrait, ahe pro- ceed d to make her toilet for the afternoon. Her dusky hair, smoothly brushed, clustered in tiny rings close to her small head. Her black silk chess, with linen bands at throat and wrist, the former fastened by a how of cherry ribhen, completed an attire which she deemed eminently suited to Lord Trevelyan's companion and seere. tary. As she turned from the mirror she almost wished that the childlike face wore the stamp of age, and- the smooth brow was furrowed with wrinkles, for she looked, in her own eyes, alto- gether too young and undignified for her present position in life. She was standing in the oriel wirelow, thinkinp sorrowfully of the home and friends she had left, when a small handmaiden entered, bearing a tra2 on which was spread a delicate luncheon. " If you please, miss," she said with a courtesy, having deposited her burden on the table, " my lord will be pleased to see you in his room as soon as you have finished lunch. He have got ond of his tantrums bed, miss, with the gout in his foot and he bade me tell you about Saul and David, miss, which I've forgotten. • But lcastways, miss, he wants you to sing to him." " I will come down very soon," replied Giralda. " You need not stay to wait upon me," she added, with a smile that won the heart of the small maid at once and forever.

The housekeeper's assistant, too young to be counted among the family servants, withdrew in a state of ardent admiration for the new inmate and Giralda ate her tempting lunch in soli- tude.

Convinced that she had taken a right course, she resolutely repressed her rising homesickness, and nerved herself for the duties before her. De- spite his faults she was already interested in Lord Trevelyan. Despite the evidence against him, she had imbibed the housekeeper's faith in Geoffrey Trevelvan, and she was determined to use her in- fluence in his favour--supposing that she should gain any influence over the vindictive marquis. Her new duties had already a charm for her, and she felt a certain sense of responsibili4 just heavy enough to be pleasant. Having finished her lunch, she set out for the lower rooms of the eastern wing. On the stairs she encountered Negwyn, Lord Adlowe's valet, and, to her surprise, the man halted and stared at her with an earnestness that embarrassed her.

The sight of that strangely lovely face and graceful figure at Trevelyan Park aroused all the wonder and curiosity of the rctumed Aus- tralian.

Giralda, unconscious that he too had marked the strange likeness between her eyes and those of Geoffrey Trevelyan, passed on, and gained the corridor from which Lord Trevelyan's apartments opened: She knocked lightly, and the marquis himself bade her enter.

Obeying the summons, she went in. Lord lrevelyan was alone. His chair had been wheeled near the hearth, and he was amusing himself with the destruction of the great number of letters which his recent advertisement had called into existence.

He paused in his task as the young girl entered his presence, his countenance expressing his sur- prise and gratification at The bright apparition. " You are prompt, Miss Arevalo," he said. "I did not expect you for an hour yet. I supposed ladies always spent hours over their toilets. Per- haps the race is improving. It is quite time. Just draw your chair up near to mine, if you please. I shall have to defer the song I had promised myself until my nephew departs." Giralda drew a chair to the position indicated, and the old marquis looked at her pure face keenly. "My nephew, Lord Adlowe, knows you are come,"; he said. " I told him myself. He says you are an adventuress, Miss Arevalo, to answer my advertisement in person. But lam a keener judge of people than my travellei nephew. You are an unworldly, innocent, unsuspecting little creature—a mere child—and I mean that you shall be treated here with the respect which would be given my grandchild, if I had one. No one can say aught against your residence here, since Mrs. Pumpton will matronise you. Her presence is sufficient for the proprieties, although an old in- valid like me ought not to be a subject for gossip- ing tongues. You shall be my secretary, Miss AreValo. I like the name better than that of 'nurse' or companion.' And it shall be under- stood that, while my roofs shelter you, I will watch over your reputation and welfare with the jealous care of a father ! "

He spoke with an impres.sive earnestness that touched the brave little heart of the maiden.

"I thank you, my lord," she said, simply, but with a look that spoke I_,etzer than words. " I don't know much about the world, but I am sure it must be right for me to help myself and relieve mamma of my support." " The world," said the marquis, with an ironical curve of his lip, "is a den of wolves. Alam I, like you has but small chance in it,. But, then, thank Fortune, there are few lambs. Don't let Inc frighten you, child. No one -shall harm you. AL ?" he addea, as he heard a step with )rit, " I:ere conics a ,volf now."

The next moment the door opened, and his nephew, Lord Adlowe, entered the parlor. He came forward smiling, the same polished nobleman who had induced the Lady Beatrice , Hampton to consider his claims upon her hand. Innocent little Giralda, while she instinctively disliked him, wondered why his uncle called him a wolf. " Well, my gallant corsair, I suppose you are soon to be off," said the marquis. "Miss Arevalo, this gentleman is my nephen, Lord Adlowe." Giralda acknowledged the introduction with a graceful bow. Lord Adlowe forgot his politeness sufficiently to stare at her in undisguised amaze- ment, mingled with admiration for her rare beauty. "She has Geoffrey Trevelyan's eyes ?" he mut- tered, uneasily. "Ormond," said the marquis, significantly, his black eyes glittering with sudden sternness, "Miss Arevalo is my secretary. While she remains at the Park she will be to me as a daughter. You will look upon her in that light." Lord Adlowe bowed, his face flushing. He knew, what the innocent Giralda did not suspect, that those simple words contained a warning to him not to look too admiringly upon the young stranger. He sat down by the table, his face in shadow, and watched with furtive glances the new-comer. He did not like her resemblance to the cousin he supposed dead. He had a vague sense of uneasi- ness whenever he chanced to encounter her inno- cent gaze. "The carriage is ordered, and I suppose I must go, uncle," he said, consulting his watch. "I wish now that I had not decided to go to the Hampton ball. I would rather remain here—" "No doubt ! " exctaimed the marquis with keen sarcasm. "I understand you, perfectly, my dear Ormond. lam delighted with your sudden affec- tion, the more especially as you betrayed nothing similar to it during your years of absence in the East. But it is your duty to go to the ball. Lord liampton is anxious to unite the fortunes of Tre- velyan and Hampton, and I am no leas anxious to see the union accomplished. Go, then, my dear boy. I shall be down to London later in the season, and we can then renew our affectionate intercourse."

"You act as if you had no faith in me or my profe3sions 1" cried Lord Adlowe, with sudden heat.

"Well, I have none; and you know I have none," replied the marquis, coolly. "I have no faith in any one, unless in this child here. She isn't old enough to be deceitful. But faith isn't necessary to my enjoyment of your professions, Ormoud. They amuse me—they do, indeed." Lord Adlowe scowled darkly. "I hear the carriage, he said, hastily. "I have but time to catch the train. Good-bye, uncle. Adieu, Miss Arevalo."

He bowed to the latter, and shook hands with the former—an operation to which the marquis submitted with a grimace. Then he hastened from the room, muttering as he entered the wait- ing carriage: "There's some mystery shout this girl ! I mean to solve it ! By Jove ! she's a beauty, and as in- nocent as a dove 1 Strange that her eyes are so like 6colrey's ! I must inquire into her history. I'll be back up here next week, and see what I can find out from her." ( To be continued ).

GOLD " RUSHES " IN AUSTRALIA.

New " rushes "are taking place in the West Austra- lian goldfield., and according to recent information the famous Coolgardie field has been almost deserted in favour of a spot called " Pinnacles," where rich finds have been made. This locality is 58 miles north-east or Coolgardie, and is described as showing splendid alluvial ground. Another " rush " is reported at Kurualpi, where the advent of a crowd of miners has caused a great scarcity of provisions. Many diggers are about to replenish their stores with tinned 11184,S sold for from 4s. to 6s. per lb. Flour is unobtainable at £0 for 501 b. bags. At Hannan's White Feather the last news'said that there had been no tinned meat for ten &1a.% s, arid fresh meat was entir,ly unknown. The only articles on sale at the stores there being baking powder and mus- tard. The scarcity of water still continues, and the Government, in order to encourage the immediate erec- tion of condensers between Southern Cross and Cool- gardie by private enterprise, has issued a notice stating that any person sinking a shalt or well and erecting condenser will be protected by the Government. for 3.2 months from the date of starting work, and be permitted to occupy during the currency of that period is quarter of an acre for each 250 gallons of fresh water Iwr day yielded by the condenser. There has been another gold " rush " in New South Wales to a place called WN along, near Barm,dman. A newspaper correspondent writes that all the roads converging on the field are crowded with horsemen, waggons, and pedestrians. The whale vicinity of the find is one great camp, and there are now between 2,000 and 3,000 people on the ground. The gold. bearing country proves to he much more extensive than was at first supposed. A new reef hiss been discovered five. miles from the main camp, and another v-ry rich one also a considerable distance a 7 ay. The main street of Wyalong is already beginning t assume the usual characteristics of a busy old time mining carnr, butchers, bakers, and heterogeneous hawkers doing a roaring trade

THIEF-HUNTING IN THE SEWERS

There is a hand of thieves in Naples which, like rats, frequent tho underground sewers, arid horo their way into shops for the purpose of robbing tills and goods. On the morning of the 2:1(1,a leather dEaler,on unlocking his warehouse, found a large hole in the florin,, and skins and money gone to the value of three thousand francs. He called the police, and several of them, t ,ge- ther with some sewermen, penetrated into the dark vaults,lipping anti sliding along the mud of the sewers. and with a single lantern. They had not gone far when they discovered a man, and cAlled to him to stop. But with a cry, "Madonna! Don't kill me," the im,n fled along the sewer,the police firing a tter him,and following him for at least a mile, passing under three or four streets,but without ..Aiccess. The police are now watch- ing the sewers like cats ; but there are many escape - holes.

heard ,and what were now discords might be harmonious to ears of ampler scope. Music was the very voice of the , Christian Church. The hymn that was sung in the upper room at .Irrasalena nearly IGOO years ago,before the Lord went out to agony and betrayal, was the prelude of the ever-increasing harmonies which had celebrated His name and glory,and the redemption of humanity through His humiliation and death. From that hymn had grown the present wealth and glory of music, and its very en- richment and development were a tribute to His cross and passion. It was said of Luther by the Papists that be did them more harm in his songs than his sermons. Let them learn his power. It was not only by the very artistic but by the homely and hearty praise that they would best sustain worship. Praise should be the out- come of Christ's Dower in the. heart. Too much attention could not be paid by choirs to their work. The praise portion was of the highest interest, and those whose voices were tuneful should seek to render their due meed of help. All choirs and congregations had much to un- learn and yet to learn. The praise part had often been left to go cri its own legs. But it was not always so,and it was not so now in all places. When choir and con- gregation understood more Mlv than ever that praise was comely before God,that it had its own distinct place in the eervice,and that its faithful and harmonious ren- dering was inspiring to men and women,a new era would begin.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT

,VRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on Friday evenirg, Mr. VERCENT asked further-questions respecting the import into this country of prison-made goods from Germany. Mr. MUNDELLA aid the Government were inquiring into the matter, and further remarkea that an agitation was going on in Germany against the competition of orison labour, and the German Government had ordered a full inquiry into the subject. The debate on the motion for the second reading of the Registration Bill and Sir E. Clarke's amendment in favour of redistribution was resumed by Mr. •GIIAMBER- LAIN, who said the real authors of the bill were the leaders of the Liberal professional organisations, who saw their advantage in a change which would not be to the advantage of their employers or the country. There was no anomaly touched by the bill to compare with the great anomaly of the present distribution of seats. The Government knew where the most glaring evils were, and all they had to do was to insert4s, provision in the bill taking away 23 members from Ireland and giving them to the other portions of the kingdom. Mr. JOHN MORLEY ,closed the debate. He said the Govern- ment were not bound by anything they did last year t.:, admit that the4risli representation ougtit to be reduced. unless Parliament altered the whole system of Irish government. If we were to have redistribution, we must have it thoroughly. On the division Sir E. Clarke's amendment was rejected by a 32 to 278, and the second reading of the bill was agreed to without further division.

MONDAY.

In the Hous,,e of Commons on Monday evening, on the order for the second reading of the Budget Bill, Mr. J. G. LAWSON moved that the bill be read a second time that day six months. Ile said its crovisions would injure every man in the country who occupied a yard of land. Mr.,l3oNson seconded the amendment. He said that in no instance did the interest oßtrewers' capital, after deducting goodwill, exceed 7 per -cent, while the interest earned by the large binks averaged in per cent. If the increased beer duty was persisted itT, the brewer would be compelled to resort to the use of the cheap foreign produce known as raw grain. Mr. EVERETTIES a farmer and small landowcer,congratulated the Chancellor of the Exchequer on his;just and fair pro- posals. Mr. LONG, OG behalf of the agriculturists, opposed ithe Chancellor of the Exclivuer's proposals, arid Mr. SEAW-LEFEVRE defended them. The debate was adjourned at midnight.

In the House of Lords Lord Hear of AVALON called attention to the state of the navy, and advocated addi- tional shipbuilding and an increase of officers and men. Earl SPISMCEIt declared that the Government were fully alive to the importance of this subsecr. He thought it was not for the advantage of the country that they should make public the whole of their scheme in ally one year. At the present moment we were suffering from the competition of other nations consequent on publicity of intentions as shown by the annual navy estimates. Steps had beea taken to increase the number of officers and men.

Sir Louis Mallet, a good Radi- cal, had, he said, described graduatien as an economic heresy which had threatened the foundations of society on the Continent, yet this heresy is now the foundation stone of the Budget of the Chancellor of the Exchequer. Mr. BUXTON said they had heard a great deal regarding the agricultural interest, but they haq,h,ard no objec- tkin made tattle Government proposLls affecting settled personalty and urban realty, which were mu3li more seriously affee,ted by the bill. ACCIDENT TO A LOCAL ANGLER.

A. somewhat serious accident happened on Satur- day evening, to Mr. C. Basketer, of Milner-street, a well-known and ardent Warrington fisherman, while engaged in his favourite pastime at Rossett. /t appears that while moving from cne spot to another he slipped, and in trying to save himself fell with his leg under him, breaking it below the knee. He was conveyed home early on Sunday morning, and is now progressing favourably.

OF PADGA.TE.

The Rev. Richard Appleton, brother to Captain Appleton. of Bruche Hall, Padgate, and fellow and tutor of Trinity College, Cambridge, has been ap- poioted to th. Vicarage of St. George's, Camberwell, about to be vacated by the Rev. Norman Campbell. This is the parish in which the late Rev. Arthur Appleton officiated as curate until the date of his death.

PADGATE SICK CLUB.

The balance sheet of this club for the year 1893 has just been published. The balance in hand on the 31st December, 1892, was £242 163. 2d.. and the members' payments for 1893 amount to £2B 9s. 91., and the total income to £277 6s. 11d. Payments have been made for members' sick pay and on death of members during the past year amounting to £2l 35., and there was on the '3lst December last a balance of 1.255 is. 10d. in the hands of the trea- surer.

An organ recital was given in St. Paul's Church, on Tuesday evening, by Mr. 11/41artin Schneider, of Liverpool, in aid of the fund which is being raised to enlarge the vestry, which has long been found to be utterly inadequate for the accommodation of the clergy and choir. An entertainment was given on Monday evening by the Bank-street Mission Minstrel in the National Schools, Church-street, in aid of the build- ing fund of St. George's Mission. The Rector (the Rev. F W. 'Willis) presided. An excellent pro- gramme was submitted, songs being given by Messrs. J. W. Taylor, George Caldwell, E. Butter- worth, P. H. Simkin, F. Harding, A. Simkin, T. Hayes, W. FM-MK-COXING DEPARTURE OF THE REV. A. V. C. H ORDERN.

The "St. .James' Church, Latchford, Monthly Magazine" in a reference to the Rev. A. V. C. Hor- dern, curate, states :--" For a long time it has been an open secret that his ambition has always been to become an Army chaplain, and now he has at- tained the :first step towards the fulfilment of his wishes. The Chaplain-General of her Majesty's forces has appointed him to the post of assistant chaplaita at Gosport, a suburb of Portsmouth ; and he is to leave for his new duties at the end of this month:We shall all miss him very much,and though of course we hope soon to have the services of an- other curate, it will be a difficult matter indeed to find one who will carry on the work with the whole-hearted .-zeal which has characterised Mr. Horderia, or one who will so quickly endear himself to every class of our people as he has done. How- ever, though .we shall greatly regret his' departure, we.shall 'eel that his promotion is richly deserved, and he will carry with him our heartiest wishes for his happiness and success in his new sphere of la- bour amongst our soldiers."

SEMOITS FIRETN GOLBORNE-STREET.

Information was received at the Fire station at 11-25 onWalnesday last wee::,that a fire had broken out at the basket and hamper manufactory of Alderman Hutchinson,.Golborne-street. The build- ing is a three-storied one and the upper storey is used as a workshop by Mr. Mawson, tailor, of Sankey-street. The brigade, under Superintendent Turner, at once proceeded with the hose cart, and in a short time were playing on the flames with two powerful branches,from hydrants in the street. There was a good supply of water and after two hours' hard work the fire was got under control. The second floor and a large stock of baskets and hampers were completely destroyed,as were also the contents of Mr. Mawson's workshop, which con- tained a large quantity of Tfinished and unfinished articles of clothing. Unfortunately Mr. Mawson's loss exceeding .Elsoi notcovered by insurance. The damage to Alderman Hutchinson's stock, and the property will be considerable. The main body of the brigade withdrew about two o'clock. and two firemen were left to watch the premises. At about half-past two the fire broke out again, but it was soon subdued by the men in charge. The steam fire engine Major was also kept in readiness with steam up in case it should have been wanted.

WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL FINANCE.

A REJOINDER FROM COUNCILLOR SHAW GREEN.

THE QUESTION REVIEWED.

As there were so many points of difference be- tween the opinions of Councillor Shaw Green and those of the gentleman whose views we published under the title of" Municipal Finance; some critic- isms and suggestions," an " Examiner" reporter this week again called upon Councillor Shaw Green to see if he would like to add to the argument he had already advanced. Mr. Green said that in read- ing the article of last week, he had not found any- thing to alter in the slightest degree, his previous views. "We both desire the same end," he added, "though we have widely different ways of looking ' at and approaching it. In venturing some further replies I should like to clear the ground somewhat. The gentleman whose views you published,to whom for brevity I will refer to as Mr. 8., advanced four opinions :—(1) That the Warrington Corporation should lay a cable roadway. (2) That the Corpora- tion should at once take down the buildings in the narrow streets, widen the same, and erect new buildings on the sites. (3) That the Corporation should • consolidate its debts and get the tire ex- tended for the loans to run,and obtain the money on cheaper terms. (4) That the rateable value of the property in the borough was too low. "In seeking to:prove his opinions, Mr. B. makes certain statements which,l ventured to controvert, and I regret that some of them re-appeared in his views last week in an aggravated form. Mr. B. also makes what I look upon as charges against the Corporation of not being wide-awake to the require- ments of the town, which everyone is proud to re- cognise' is growing rapidly, and also of wanting skill in the conduct of the financial affairs of the borough. Had he stuck to and argued in favour of his theory of the Corporation putting down a cable track, I would not have ventured to say any. thing, but I felt that it was proper that the ratepayers should have a definite reply clearing up some of the statements from one of those entrusted with the care of the finan- ces. To refer to the proposition as to the trams, I repeat that at present it is not possible for the Borough to undertake any such duty, and there- fore I hold that I am correct in describing the pro- ject as more of a pleasant dream than a possible reality. I admit that some day, and the sooner the better, it will be an actual reality, and I hope to live to take part in bringing it about. Nothing that I have said can be construed into suggesting that steps should not be taken to widen our streets and improve our chief thoroughfares. The rate- payers know how we have been looking ahead, pur- chasing property here and there as opportunity offered. Our policy is a forward one, but of a wise and careful character. Some people say we are go- ing too fast now, whilst others say we are not go- ing fast enough. Let Mr. B. look again at our annual statement, and calculate what it cost to widen the lower half of Bridge-street, and then let him ask himself whether he would face the rate- payers of the borough with a demand such as his scheme would require. I can assure him and all others that Mr. Alderman Wright and his com- mittee have these improvements constantly before them, and if some kind friend would make the town a present of £50,000 they would soon prove that their plan was ready and only awaiting the money to put it into operation." "There is the question of the consolidation of the debt." "Let me clear up first the argument referring to the borrowing by the Corporation. After my clear statement I was surprised to see it brought up again. I repeat in unmistakable terms we are not borrowing money at 31 per cent. We are borrow- ing for the present at ..E3 2s. 63. 117 c do not lend moneg at tzll and the comparison between buying iron at per ton and selling it at .E 3 is altogether beside the question and positively unfair and mis- leading. To make the matter so clear that it need not be referred to again, the very small sum that we have had to invest is put in consols not willingly but because we are required by the Government, and the same apflies to the savings of the Police Superannuation Fund. We would much have pre- ferred taking it upon our own bonds but we were not permitted. "The next matter I want to clear up is the state- ment that the Corporation is altogether too gener- ous with the ratepayers money. The story that embellishes this statement is very funny but not appropriate. The Committee has gradually reduced the rate of interest without cost and inconvenience and it will continue to follow that safe course. It would be easy to make ducks and drakes of our finances but to those who know what finaneing means a wise policy is always a safe one. We are told that people would be glad to invest their money at 21 per cent. Would they? Stockport last week issued a 3 per cent stock. Why did they not issue a 21 one if people are so ready to invest as suggested? Why did not Liver- pool and Manchester and other towns do so? Nay, can Mr. B. point to a single Corporation as an illustration. Then will he be pleased to tell us how much Stockport Council paid for the issu- ing of their stock, we :litios,v something of these matters, and I think the ratepayers will trust us. I pointed out that we are now engaged upon a scheme for the consolidation of our loans with the intention of issuing a debenture stock. Surely this ought to be sufficient for the present. We shall have to go to Parliament and we expect to deposit our bill next session." " What about the annuties?" "I stated that a very large part of our capital indebtedness is the capital value of the gas and water annuities. As to the latter, Parliament has fixed the maximum redemption value, but with respect to the gas annuities it is not so, for with every reduction in the rate of interest these have naturally increased in value. I said the Corporation is fully alive to the ad visibility of redeeming these for it is well known we have been purchasing them whenever we had the oppor- tunity," "But Mr. B. says these can easily be effected by a special act." " Yes, so he says; but it is not so easy as it looks. Parliament will not take the view quite so easily as it is put. In consequence of there being no limit fixed we shall have to make such arrangements for

their redemption as Parliamentwill sanction. Some people go so far as to say that Parliamentwill make no arrangement but we are sanguine enough to be- lieve it will. The matter is not new. It has been discussed in the Council and I had the duty of sub- mitting the resolution that we do go to Parliament for power to compels the owner to sell and this will be in the new Bill."

"What about Mr. B.'s reply to your observations as to the time allowed for redeeming the cost of the waterworks ?"

" I repeat emphatically my statement that we made a big fight,and as I was present, I speak from knowledge, and I venture to say that I differ as to any ratepayers thinkinglwe made a "remarkably small one." The House of Commons was the court, and we as the app:icants got all we could, and we got the terms of repayment as well as the time so arranged that we were well satisfied with the re- sult. When we go again we shall have full infor- mation as to what has been done elsewhere, and we shall strive for all that ought to be got." "With respect to the rateable value of the borough: have you anything further to add?" "I do not question the statement that there may be here and there an odd case of under-assessment. I question the application of the "rule of thumb" against actual results based upon definite informa- tion. I say whatever the general statistics say it is elsewhere, and if applied here, Warrington ought to be so much, is not worth consideration when the actual letting values which is the only basis of valuation, is ascertained. Our Overseers and the Assessment Committee have every means, even the help of the income tax assessment at their com- mand, of getting true information, and knowing them as I do I ventured to say that this town, though low in a general rule of thumb estimate is assessed upon its letting value." "But an instance of under-assessment was ad- duced?"

"The illustration is as unwisely chosen as it could be. Opinioms differ, and differ seriously as to the way in which public-houses should be assessed, and I would commend the illustration supplied by Mr. B. to the attention of the Assessment Committee. I could myself supply other similar cases, but all of them that class of property. "Just a few words to sum up the whole matter. I fully endorse everything relating to the geograpi- cal position of the town and its possiblities. Igo further and say that the congestion of our streets must be grappled with—that the bridge over the Mersey must be widened; that the level crossing at Wilderspool should be altered. The two latter be- ing almost as important as any other part of our improvement schemes. But will a rate at present inclusive of the water rate equal to 7s. Bd. in the X,. I submit the time has not yet arrived, that it is coming, and that we are making ready—anticipat- ing the day—l wish all who read this answer to accept."

JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

MINERS AND THE WAGES QUESTION.

MEETING OF TIIE FEDERATION.

A special meeting of the Executive Council of the Miners' Federation was held in St. Martin's Town Hall, London, on Wednesday, to consider the Scot- tish wages question. Mr. Woods' M.P., vice presi- dent, presided, in the absence or Mr. Pickard, who had started for Berlin, and there were present Messrs. E. Cower, W. Parrott (Yorkshire), 0. Haslam (Derbyshire), W. Bailey (Nottingham), E. Edwards (Staffordshire), J. Haslam (Derbyshire), J. Williams (North Wales), T. Ashton (secretary), J. Glover (Lanca- shire). J. Weir (Scotland), W. Whitfield (Bristol), A. Stanley (Cannock Chase),and J. Batchelor (Coal Porters' Union). In accordance with the instructions of the Scottish Miners Federation at their meeting at Glasgow on Tuesday, Mr. Smillie, of the West of Scotland, re- ported to the Executive that the coalowners in that dis- trict had demanded a reduction of is. per day in miners' wages, and that in Ayrshire 7,500, in Lanarkshire 27,000 and In Mid and %Vest Lothian 500 men had received notice and had come out of the pits. After full considera- tion of Mr. Swillie's statement the Executive agreed to advise the leaders of the Scottish miners to enter into negotiations with their employers with a view to the withdrawal of the notices for a reduction and in the meantime a general conference of all federated districts will be convened to consider the reply of the Scottish coalowners and the wages question generally. Prior to the Executive meeting a general assembly of the delegates appointed to attend the Berlin Conference was held, and arrangements made for 23 delegates from the Federation to start on Thursday morning from Charing Cross. The Federation delegates are hopeful of securing the adhesion of the International Conference to an international restrictive policy and the eight-hour day.

LYMM.

TUE PROPOSED FORMATION OF WARDS.—The Cheshire County Council have decided to send a committee to Lymm on the 24th inst. to hold an inquiry at the Tech- nical Institute, Bridgewater-street, to take into consid- eration the proposal of the Local Board/or the division of the district into four wards, and for increasing the members of the Board from nine to ten.

DEATH OF MR. HUGH CAMF.RON.—Mr. Hugh Cameron, of Lymm, died at half-past two on Tuesday afternoon. The deceased gentleman had been ailing for about seventeen weeks, and had during that time been attended by Dr. Wright. of Broom Edge. On Sunday he had a relapse and became unconscious, remaining so until his death. For seventeen years he was a member of the Lymm Local Board, and for eleven years officiated as chairman, in which latter capacity paitieularly he rendered valuable services to the ratepayers. He was agent for the late Mr. J. C. Dewhurst for 25 years in Lymm, and seven years in Scotland. Mr.Cameron,who was highly respected and well-known in the district,was 58 years of age, and leaves a wife and two children. THE LATE Mn. Mr. Dewlmrst, bequeaths to his grand- son, George Littleton Dewhorst £3,000, and lea vies to him the advowsnn of St. Peter's. together with the site of the church, the schools and the schoolmaster's house, and the honse at present occupied by the inneinh-nt. By a will in the Scottish form, dated October 17, 1892, the testrtor left his Aberechil and the Scottish estates upon trust for his son Robert Henry, and his first and other sons, and he left all the residue of his Eielish property in trust as to one-fifth each for his grandsons, George Littleton. Harry, Gerard, Cyril, and Willem Dewhurst, with special provisions for the retention of his capital in the firm of G. and R. Dewhurst, in which they are partners.
