TLISIS FOR THE HOLIDAY)9

THIS has 14een a week of political The Politica.' unrest—in London at all events. , Situation. We phlegmatic provincials seem to take thirgs more quietly than the excitable Cockney. One of the metropolitan evening papers has been assuring its readers, and all else whom it concerned, that Sir WILLIAM HARCOURT was on the point of resigning his position in the ministry and retiring from the political strife. This an- nouncement does not seem to have had any more serious effect than to give the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER a little amusement in the midst of his arduous Budget labours. Sir WILLIAIt declared at the National Liberal Club on Wednesday that he was still alive and so far as he know was not dying, and, furthermore, that he had no intention of deserting the Liberal party. As ho said, he has a tough fight before him for he has to meet John Barleycorn both in regard to the additional six- pence a barrel on beer and sixpence a gallon on whiskey and the Veto Bill. But he is prepared for the encounter, and he may be safely relied Upon to give a good. account of himself. According to another of the rumours that have

-

tifistOis**o

been 'floated during the week, we have been in the midst of ;political "crisis" of the moet serious chazaeter. What was to happen was nothing leas ;than an immediate dis- solution ,ofteParliament, or at the very least the re- signation of the Government. And all for what? Simply hecanze the Government have had to face an important division on the second reading of the Budget, with the niee ParneOlite members vot- ing against ,them. But there is really nothing in this to reach :the dignity of eetieie. The Govern- ment has laid ielf out from theeheginning to meet important anti feitical division, .and the Parnel- lites have so often threatened :defection that now that they have finelly decided to .champion the in- terests of the whiskey sellers in !preference to oma Rule, their .ostility has 'been discounted and reckoned for. In all other reeepects the majority of the C-avernment is coeepact and , ,ue4ted. It is the same majority which was re- turreed less than two yews ago to earry.eKet a pro- , gran=e of reforms, e. Cargo part of whieh has as yet been barely entered upon. Until itle4s pro- granulite has been fully unrolled, ad tuntil it has either been carried ,out or it has been dis- closed beyond question eer doubt that the try minority in the Henze of Commons .aled the Tory majority in the House of Lord are eesolutely determined to veto All reform, the Qavernment and the Liberal party are under no neceasity to seek a fnesh mandate from the constituencies. A nd, as a ratter of fact, no one wants a diseolution just now—not even the Tories themselves,. The b itter hostility which, under one pretence and another, is shown towards the Budget proposals is instigated by the rich and privileged classes who control the Tory Party. Although the CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER has attempted only in a very timid and tentative I fashion to apportion the burden of public taxes to the shoulders that have to bear it, yet he has gone too far for the wealthy people who have too long enjoyed an unjust inamuni ty. These people cry out loudly enough for an increase in the naval and military ermam ents of the country, yet when , the bill has to be paid they are dissatisfied and ' crumble because the whole cost is not thrown upon those classes of the population which, while least able to pay, are already taxed, directly or indirectly, out of all proportion with their means. Under the present circumstances there can be little doubt that Lord ROSEDERY has taken the right course in announcing as he did on Wednes- day that the Government intend to fight the battle to the end. Let them persist in treading the path of democratic legislation and administra- tion they have a lready fairly entered upon, and let those who will take the responsibility of thwart- ing the policy which there are many reasons for believing is cordially sanctioned by the country.

ARE we on the eve of another A Mysterious Manitcstatiou. Indian Mutiny? That is a ques-

tion which is being discussed in certain quarters, and a ome people seem divosed to answer it in the affirmative. It appears that there has lately been a rather mysterious mani- festation in parts of India, which in some respects is similar to the signs which preluded the out- break of forty years ago. At that time small cakes, known as chapatties, were distributed in a mysterious but singularly expeditious way amongst the dusky natives of the peninsula, and now the sign that has been observed by the authorities is the marking, or rather the plastering with mud, of the mango trees. As to the real significance of these latter marks there is a diversity of opinion. On one hand it is said they have no secular mean- ing, being more of the nature of religious ritual. Other interpreters say they are an indication of agrarian discontent. The most serious interpre- tation of all is that the marks are neither more nor less than an intimation to the natives to prepare for an uprising against their English rulers. The plaster is said to consist of mud mixed with the hair of the cow and the swine, and Dr. LEITNER of the lOriental Ijnivergity JPt Opstrues the mix- f, aro as follows: The mud repterfts !;1.-w • of Behar, the mango its productiveness, the Cow's hair relates to the Hindus, and the pig's hair or bristles to the Mahommedans, and the reading of the message is : " This soil of ours, whether be- longing to .Hindus or Mahommedans, is being. affected in its productiveness." If this be the correct reading of the enigma,the movement among the Indian natives is clearly agrarian rather than political, and therefore not likely to be so serious as the mutiny of 1857. At the same time if the latest telegram from Bombay giving an account of the mutinous condnct 9f some eQmptki of Bengal infantry be corn) ct, a state of affairs is developing in the great :dependency whicl.l will demand both wisdom and firmness in those who are in control there.

IF any one were to judge the state Subsidizing of thii; country by the speech which Landlord. _ _

Lord SALISBURY delivered in Wilt- shire he would come to the conclu- sion that the one suffering class in it was that of landlords. His Lordship spoke in a county which has the reputation of containing the most wretchedly paid agricultural labourers in the whole of England, yet all his sympathy was reserved for the landowners. He regretted the palmy days of Protection when rent rolls were swollen at the expense of the community, and especially of the labouring people, who had to pgy high prices for the bread they ate. In one ; respect he displayed more clear-sightedness than do some of his followers, for he declared that Protection is dead and that Free Trade is and must be the policy of this country. But he evi- dently regards the Tory Party as still an instru- ment for the advancement ,of the interests and priveleges of the landed class. As they cannot ,have • the value of their property fictitiously raised by a protective tariff he coolly suggests that they should be relieved of the, burdens in the way of rates that they have to bear. There is a candour about Lorrn SALISBURY that is beyond praise. He ' will have nothing to do with the democratic veneer with which some of his followers attempt to cover up the real policy of their party, With him as with the Tories of years gone by the main object is to aggrandise the landowners. This cannot now be done in the old rough and ready way by placing tariffs on the necessaries of life and by the wholesale grabbing of common lands, but it can be done in a more insidious though equally effective fashion by shuffling public burdens from the shoulders of landlords to those of traders and workers. When they were last in power the Tories did something in this line by their so-called subsidies in aid of local rates,.and it is quite clear from the tenor of Lord SALISBURY'S remarks that when they again hold office they will attempt to go further on the same lines. Yet, surely, if there is one class in this country which does not require State aid it is the landlords.

,1 ANNUAL CLIJRCR PARADE Or VOLUNTEERS AT RUNCORN. On :tinclay the " F " and " G " companies of the 2nd (Earl of (hester's) Volunteer Battalion Cheshire Regi- ment attended divine service at Holy Trinity Church. Ashton (in command), Captain Harrison and Captain G. F. Ashton, the chaplain (the Rev. Dr. Preston), Lieutenant Speakman, Quarter- master Sergeant Hankinson, Sergeant-Instructor Player, Colour-Sergeants Mort and Voole, Sergeants Woodland, Rushton, Joynson, Harvey, Edwards, Holmes, and Cooper; rank and file 48, hand 26, under Band Sergeant Outhwaite. Dr. Preston was a most appropriate and powerful one, the , text being Psalm cxxxvii, 1, " By the rivers of Babylon there we sat down ; yea we wept when we reinentbere:l Zion." Subsequent to the service the volunteers par- aded back to the Drill Hall where they were addressed by Colonel Ashton who offered them his congratulations upon their smart appearance, good muster, and ' improved pace.

Mr. Worsley. coroner, held an inquiry at the Runcorn Burial Board Office, Centel-Ty, on Saturday morning re- lative to the d,atli of John Hanson, 57 years of age. captain of the flat" Richard,' owned by Rochdale Canal Company. The evidence adduced was to the effect that the deceaged and his son, Beaumont Hanson arrived in the, tidal basin, Runcorn, on board the flat, at about ten o'clock on Thursday night. The son was told by his father to go below to rest,and said tilt t he would see him- self that the boat was properly berthed. The son accord- in2ly went to bed, but was awak,ned at about Vireo o'clock the next morning by a young man named John Inman shouting to him. Be got up and was surprised to find his father not on the boat. Shortly afterwards a boat hook, belonging to the flat was discovered in the water, and it.wai thought that the father had probably fallen into the water. Grappling irons were at once procured, and after a short time the body of Jihn Han- son was recovered from the water, not far from where the honk was found. Ara examination of the body was made by P.C. Booth, who found no marks of violence upon it.—ln reviewing the evidence the Coroner spoke of the necessity for the provision of public btths in Run- corn, and expres'sed his satisfaction that the sulT•ct had been taken up. He was of the opinion that should such a provision be made, there would be a considerable decrease in the number of persons drowned. The jury returned a verdict of" Accidentally drowned."

DAMAGES AGAINST THE LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY COMPANY.

On Friday, at the tVidnes County Court, before his Honour Judge Shand and a jury, a case was heard in which Andrew Quinn. pig dealer, Moss Bank,. Widoes, sued the London and North-Western Railway for £2l 83. alleged dainage for the loss of three nigs, injury to a fourth, and loas of sale of twenty-six others. Mr. Valentine D'Arcy (instructed by Mr. C. H. Lewis) repre- sented the plaintiff, and Mr. W. F. Preston tho defend- ant company. The plaintiff's case was that on the 30th of December last, a consignment of thirty pigs for him arrived at the Collingwood Dock, Liverpool, from Dun- dalk. The following day (Sunday), the plaintiff's sun went down and took the pigs to the Waterloo Goods Station of the London and North-Western Railway Company, where the animals were put.intotrocks. lie was informed that the pigs would be sent to Widnes at four o'clock the following morning, but they did not arrive until the Tuesday morning, in consequence of which the plaintiff lost his market. for the pigs. When they did arrive three of the animals were- found dca,d in the truck, and a fourth one was so much injured that that the plaintiff had to sell it at half pric,,. The CALI ic! of death was stated by two experts to he duo to starva- tion, gauged by the length of time in transit. The de- fence was that the Waterloo Goods Stair-al was tinted to traffic on Sundays ; also that the animals wor.- sent to Widnes by the first possible train. With regard to the starvation of the pigs, it was held that t hero was pro- vender provided at Watertoo Station, and the plaintini son could have fed the pigs when there nit Sund:ty if In' had chosen. The Judge held that the plaintiff could not, recover for the loss of sale, as there wa a no epeciiic agreement with the complus, al to the delivery of tht, pits. On the ()tiler points the jury returned a vpr,..lH for the plaintiff for the full amount allowed hy law for the four pigs (2,2 'each). Judgment was entered for -.EIB and costs, and his Honour granted plaintiff counsera fees owing- to the questions of law involved.

UNRIDOE-STRERT GOSPEL MISSION, RUNCORN.

The anniversary services in eonnecti(;l3 with the above Mission took place on Sunday, when two sermons were delivered, one in the morning and the other in the even- ing, by Mr. Rote, of Liverpool, ex-president of the Free , Gospel Churches. On Monday evening a service of song entitled " Eva " was rendered by the choir, conducted by Mr. MoorAeld, the readings being undertaken by Mr. Backhouge, of Liverpool. The annual meeting was held on Tuesday evening, Mr. Chadwick occupying the chair. The report etatal that the Mission had an adversu balance, but that good work was being carried on in the neighbourhood, and hopes were entert tined that when the trade of the tows brightened, the financial condition of the Mission would improve. The meeting was after- wards addressed by tbe Revs. J. Pulford Williams, T. Caseley and W. Albert.

RC'SICORN MERSEY MISSION EXCURSION TO LIVERPOOL AND

NEw

From a reference to our advertising columns it will be seen that the annual excursion, in connection with the Runcorn Branch of the Mersey Mission to Seamen, will take place as usual on Whit-Tuesday when a journey will be made, on board the saloon steamer" Crocus," to Liverpool and New Brighton. The vessel will leave the Big House, Bottom Locks, Runcorn, at 8.30 a,rn. prompt and a call will be made on the way at the tiaining ship 1" Indefatigable," which will give excursionists fifteen minut2s to view this fine vessel. The fare is extremety low having been reduced to is. 3d; return. This small amount includes admission into the Sailor's Home, Liverpool, and by showing their tickets at the Palace, New Brighton, visitors travelling by Vie "Crocus " will he admitted at a reduced charge. The Mission drum and fife band will accompany the party on their trip, and at intervals will play selections of choice music. SMALLPDX AT Sr. HELENS.

NEW Id9c. 4/4 JDt7TfltitS. It is :,,attO that the old copper works at Bold, St. Helens, whia have been standing ifle since they were vacated by a firm of manure manufacturers, are about to be started by a company managed by a Birmingham gentleman, for the manufacture of steel tubes for locomotives. Notice, it is said, has been given to the London and North-Western Rail- way Company for the siding to be ready for use hy the 18th inst. It is expected that very shortly 500 hands will be employed, and this number may be increased. It is also interesting to know that bor- ing operations were on Wednesday commenced at Abbot's Hall Farm, Gorsey-lane, on the Bold Hall Estate, St. Helens. Some excellent seams of coal

are believed to exist at a great depth. The opera- tions have been ordered by the syndicate who pur- chased the estate, but on the discovery of minerals underneath it is probable leases will be granted to other firms to work them.

PRESBYTEItIANIS-M IN RUNCORN.

SKETCHES OF THE OLD BUILDING AND THE

NEW. • The valedictory services in the old chapel will be held on Sunday morning and evening as announced in our advertising columns, and at this juncture it might prove interesting to many Runcurnians, more especially those of the Presbyterian persua- sion, to know a little of the past history of the old building, of which they are about to take a last farewell. The chapel was erected in the year 1818, and was connected with the Countess of Hunting- den's Chapel, its services being conducted on a similar principle to that of the Church of England. Raving been instrumental in doing a great amount, of good in the locality, for thirty years the chapel was transferred. in May,1849, to the Presbyterian denomina- tion, the Rev. William Roberts, D.D., Holyhead, un- dertaking the pastorate. After a successful ministry of six years, the Rev. William Roberts accepted a call from the Welsh Church in New York to take over the pastor- ate. He was succeeded by the Rev. John Davies, of Kenchester, who remained for nearly three years, and was the means of doing much good work. The Rev, W. Evans, M.A., was the next minister, and he laboured !for his flock with considerable success for about eight years. The chapel house was built during this gentle- man's ministry, and has proved of great service since. ' In 1867 the Rev. EvantWilliaEris was called to labour amongst his Presbyterian brethren in Runcorn with whom he continued to work for more than a generation. Since 1849 the church, year by year, gradually grew stronger until in 1872 the yearly grants which had been made were not required, and the church thus became self-supperting. In that year the building was reno- vated and re-seated at a cost of £345 ; and in 1881-82. two new classrooms were built, and other improvements effected. In 1887 the Rev. Evan Williams was forced to leave Runcorn for Australia, but in the summer of 180 the Rev. J. Pulford Williams, the present pastor, visited the church, and the congregation formed such a high opinion of him that they asked him to take the over- sight. He consented, and his since laboured zealously for the cause with much success. For seine time past there has been a general feeling that new and more commodious premises were urgently needed. This was occasioned bi the unsuitability of the present buildines, both as regards structure and posi- tion and the lack of accommodation,the room at the dis- poBal of the Sunday School being especially inadequate for its growing needs. Considerable difficulty was ex- perienced in. ohaining a suitable site, hut the committee have at length been fortunate enough to secure, at a reasonable cost, a plot of land facing Victoria-road, one of the new and most important thorone,litares in the town, situate in the midst of an increasing population, whilst not too far removed from the inesent premises. The church in course of erection will seat €5O and the school premises will aeeommodate 400, in. addition to which, class rooms will be provided for the senior and infant scholars. The whole scheme, inclusive of the land, is estimated to cost about £4,500; and the enthu- siesm with which the matter has been taken op augurs well for its success. The congregation however, is com- posed principally of the working class, and an earnest appeal is therefore made for assistance.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY OUR OCCA.SIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

--- The controversial question of keeping the Ship Canal Company to their original promise to con- struct a graving dock at Runcorn, instead of allowing them to forego it, and spend he money in another direction, is still claiming the major por- tion of the attention of the traders in the town. It is understood that the General Purposes Com- mittee, in whose capable hands the matter has been placed, have, up to the present time, received no reply from the Directors of the Company. The opinion of the carriers in Runcorn, as stated in a previous issue of the "Examiner," is that by ex- pending the money in the extension of lay-byes and warehouses at Weston Point would prove a, more powerful inducement to trade than the con- struction for a graving dock. Then when the trade had returned to the town, money could safely Ite spent in laying down slip ways and other re- quirements for the repairing of vessels. This view certainly appears at first sight rather feasible, but judgment must be reserved until a little more con- sideration has .been krought to bear upon the matter. The shipwrights in the town are indeed deserving of sympathy. Before the construction of the canal there were several yards on the banks of the river fitted up with all necessaries for the re- pairing of large vessels, and these were the means of employing more than two hundred workpeople. Then came the construction of the Ship Canal which swept away, in its route, all these yards and slip ways, a promise being extracted from the Company to construct a graving dock as a sort of compensa- tion for the devastation they had cause,d. Of course,the shipwright's trade has consequently con- siderably diminished because there is no provision for the repairing of large craft,and as a result blips are driven to other places.

Whatever might be said to the contrary this state of things is to be regretted and the shipping com- munity of Runcorn express the opinion that, if the company are relcased from their promise, and spend the money in other works, the trade will receive its death blow. This side of the question will, lam sure, be treated by the members of the General Purposes Committee in a disinterested manner and they will probably not lose sight of the fact that some prominent shipwrights state that a sum of from £4,000 to £5,000 would be amply sufiicient to meet their present requirements in the form of a good substantial slipway, eliotild the idea of a grav- ing dock be ahundonr_4l,

A rumour has been circulated, and has had the effect of darkening many prospects, that the United Alkali Company were closing their works at Whit- suntide for a term of several weeks. The general depression of trade is known by almost everyone and consequently such an assertion as the above, found many believers. It is, however, with the greatest of pleasure that I am able to state on good authority that such a cessation of operations will not take place, but that the holidays will only last throughout Whitsun week, work being resumed on the following Kniday.

An old Runcornian chancing to visit the Runcorn docks within the present week would not, have failed to have been struck literally by the large number of vessels loading and discharging therein. The docks presented quite a busy scene which was a reliable evidence of the steadily increasing trade of the port. This will be very welcome news to many and it is to be hoped that such a state of thinp. \via continuo, awl that the dark clouds of depression which have been hovering above us for so many months witl ero long be displaced by the bright rays of prosperity.

Whitsuntide is again upon us and the opportu- nities of pleasantly spending a ;lion relaxeion of , duty are many and various. At the time of writ- ing the prospect of having a bright Whitsuntii.l,2 is very uncertain, rain having fallen for the greater part of the .week. However, let us hope that that mysterious and neverseen individual, the clerk of the weather, will resery e his copious showers for a few days in order that those who are able might have an opportunity of enjoying themselves. The railway companies are as usual running cheap ex- cursions to many of the most prominent and favoured cf pleasure resorts, and there is every reit- Son to believe, that should the weather be tine, many will take advantage of them. In Runcorn, the customary processions of friendly societies, arid Sunday school children will take place on Whit- Monday ; the former in the morning and the latter at noon.

The auspicious occasion of the opening of the Manchester Ship Canal by her Majesty the Queen is near at hand. This being so, it might prove of interest to state that the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners have received an invitation from the directors or the cornpAny to attend the ceremony. A steamer will be placed at their disposal if the invitation is accepted, and the arrangements for their reception are definite and convenient.

SINGERS AND FLOWERS.

If a French specialist, M. Joal, is right, admirers of fashionable singers and actresses wi.ll do well not t;') per- • sist in loading them with -wreaths and bow-pmts. The smell of flowers, says M. Joal, is injurious to the voice. The rose, and all flowers with very strong scent, are especially to be avoided, he says, hut personal suseep_ tibility, he thinks, has much to do with the matter. Ho knows operatic singers who have complett-ly lost their voicrs through thnir passion for certain flowers. To some the violet is especially injurious ; others would do well to avoid lilac, and other again should strickly taboo the harmless-looking, gardenia. The advice, however, . would certainly be more practical if the alarmi,,t would condescend -to tell us which particular people come into the different categories enumerated. "

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ASSAULTS ON THE POLICE.

Peter Hare and George Flannagan, both of Howard- street, Sankey Bridges, were each claarged at the War- rington Borough Court on Monday, with being drunk and disorderly on Saturday night in Orford-lane and also with assaulting P.Cs. Clough and Bell. The evidence showed that the prisoners were going towards Orf,,rd Barracks, and making use of filthy language ta passers by. The officers stopped them and asked for their names and addresses, but they refused to give them, and the officers then arrested Hares. The prisoner commenced to kick his captors about the legs and the second prisoner also did his best to get Hares away. P.C. Bell then got hold of Flannagan, and tly- two officers and prisoners were struggling on the ground for some time until P.C. Barnett came to the assistance of the officers, and the prisoners were locked up. The magistrates decided to send Hares to gaol for one month, and fined Flannagan 103. and costs or 14 days' imprisonment.—Thomas Kelly was also charged with being drunk and disorderly and with assaulting P.Cs. Cooper and Marsh in Buttermarket street on Saturday night. The prisoner's b±haviour was somewhat similar to the prisoners in the previous case, and when he was arrested, he kicked Cooper and Marsh on the legs, and bit each of them severely on the fingers. He was sent to gaol for one month.

A NOISY SERVANT.

Ann Morgan,a kitchen maid at the Patten Arms Hotel, was charged at the Warrington B )rough Court on Monday with being drunk and disorderly on Sunday night. and also with assaulting Mr. Kiernan the landlord. Inspec- tor Cox stated that P.C. Turner was called into the Patten Arms Hotel on S iturday night t ) take the prisoner in charge in con3,2evience of her disorderly la?, !saviour, and he took her to the police station in a Cab. As this was an illegal arrest owing to the prisoner resid- ing on the premises of the Patten Arms, he turned her out again. He (Cox) went out some time afterwards and when in Parker-street he found her drunk again. He then locked her up.—Mr. Kiernan said that there was a disturbance in the kitchen on Saturday night, between the prisoner and the cook who were both drunk. He went to them and told them to be quiet, but soon after- wards they began again. They used very violent lang- uage, and commenced shouting very loudly, and he had to bring in a policeman to assist him to take the prisoner out. The prisoner attempted to strike him as she was being taken out, but he warded off the blow. The cook became,quiet after the prisoner had been taken out. He did not wish to press the case, as the prisoner had been a good servant, but could not keep from drink when she had money.—The magistrates dismissed the case, and the prisoner subsequently promised Mr. Taylor, the police court missionary to go to the Women's Shelter at Liverpool.

FLOGGING A HORSE.

At the Warrington Borough Court, on Friday, before Alderman Wright, Councillor Roberts, and Messrs. Wm. Pierpoint and J. W. Hayward, David Cooke, Knutsford- road, was charged With beating and ill-treating, ahusing and torturing a horse on the 23rd April. Dr. Gornall stated that on the above date, he was walking along Knutsford-road to Warrington, at about 9.15 a.m., and when opposite Tunstall's-row he saw a waggonette standing, laden with people, at the end of the row. The defendant, wko was driving, was hitting the horse with the butt end of the whip to get it to move, and got it near to Barry-street where a man went forward and got hold of the horse and led it towards Barry-street. The driver hit the animal most unmercifully in the ribs with the whip. While witness was standing by, a lad remarked that the animal might well not be able to go, as it had done 24 miles that morning. Witness con- sidered that the driver had ill-used the hotse and he gave notice to the first policeman he met.—P.C. Bell said that from what Dr. Gornall told him. he went after the defendant, but before he caught him he had got to the Stables. Upon examining the horse he found it was covered with perspiration and was trembling as if it had been ill-used. Th,re were small swellings on the ribs.— The defendant said that he had been to Hoo Green with the horse which was a jibher, but it had gone along all right until he arrived in Knutsford-road, where he had to beat it to make it go.—Defendant was fined 103. and Costs.

KATE GERRARD AGAIN!

On Saturday last a woman named Kate Gerrard,well- known to the Warringtor.is police coinpleted a term of imprisonment for being drunk and disorderly. She was again brought before the Warrington magistrates on Tuesday morning charged with assaulting and beating a neighbour named Mary Griffiths on the previous Satur- day. Mr. A. Browne who prosecuted stated that the neighbourhood where the defendant lived was in a con- stant state of uproar through the defendant's conduct. On Saturday when she returned from jail on passing the complainant's house in Hopwood-street, she went to the complainant and without any provocation struck her in the face. Immediately after that she came out of her house in Hopwood-street and threw a bucket of water on the complainant's windows. No notice was taken of it by the complainant, but when she went out later in the day the defendant again assaulted her. She struck her between the eyes, giving her.ia black eye and caus- ing biood to flow. The defendant was such a nuisance that constables had to stop in the street on Saturday afternoon to keep her quiet. He (Mr. Browne) asked for the defendant to be dealt with severely for the peace of the neighbourhood. Mrs. Griffiths. the complainant, Mrs. E. Hankey, 9, Hopwood-street, and Mrs. Ashworth, 18, General-street, corroborated Mr. Browne's statement Uto the assault. D.S. Monaghan said he visited the defendant's house the previous day. He foued her drinking with two other women. The house was in a filthy condition and her husband told him that he gave her 303. per week. The magistrates committed her to prison for two months hard labour. TRH CARRIAGE CO.'S HORSES AGAIN. Fdward Holt, bus briver, and James Kenrick, man- ager for the Warrington Carriage Company, were each summoned before the Warrington magistrates on Friday, the former charged with working and the latter with caus- ing a horse to be worked while in an unfit state on April 23. Inspector Luff said that at about 745 p.m. on the above date, he was in Bridge-st., and saw Holt driving a horse attached to a single horse 'bus. The horse was very lame in the hind legs. Witness followed the 'bus to Corporation-at-, and there examined the horse. He found that the back tendons were strained and the ani- mal was suffering from "grease." The horse was taken to the stables. He went to the stables afterwards and when taken out, the horse could scarcely hobble about. On the Wednesday after, witness went to the stables again in company with Mr. Dixon, veterinary surgeon, but Mr. Kenrick refused to let Mr. Dixon see the horse. Witness told him that if he did not let Mr. Dixon see the horse he would tell the magistrates, but Mr. Kenrick replied that he could tell the magistrates whatever he liked as he would make no defence.-I'.C. Evans corro- borated the last witness.—Mr. Kenrick said that he objected to Mr. Dixon examining his horses, and in pre- vious cases he had offered to pay half the expenses to bring any veterinary surgeon frcm Liverpool or Man-

chester to examine them. Ha could not get any veterinary surgeon in Warrington to come because they did not care to come. The horse in question had been Bold since April 23rd.—The magistrates did not consider the driver to be responsible, and Mr. W. Pierpoint said that he believed that Johnson, who had been summoned previously for a similar offence had been discharged by the Carriage Co. He suspected that it had somethingto do with appearing at the court in cases like the present. Mr. Kenrick said that was all assumption. He could tell the magistrates what Johnson was dismissed for.— The case against Holt was withdrawn, and Kenrick was fined 103. and costs. In answer to the magistrates. Bolt said that he would not have any of his wages stopped for appearing that morning at the court.

SERIOUS CHARGE AGAINST A WARRINGTON WOMAN.— Catherine Keary or Taylor was charged with having on the 30th March, 1886, kept a brothel in contravention of the Criminal Act. Inspector Cox said the prisoner in March, 1886, lived at 8, Berry's-yard, and kept a bro- thel. She was then a widow, and earned her living by prostitution. In company with Detective Monaghan he visited the house at 4.15 a.m. on the date named, and found several persons there. There were several com- plaints about the prisoner's conduct at the time. A sum- mons was issuel against the prisoner, but she did not appear, and a warrant was granted. D.S. Monaghan corroborated, and stated that the prisoner had come back to live in Warrington again within the last few days, and was living in Tilley-sr,. Chief Constable Hunt said the prisoner came to his office to obtain a licence to hawk. He suspected her of being the woman against whom a warrant was issued. The prisoner said she was only nineteen years of age at the time of the offence, and was married four years ago at Nantwich to a man named Benjamin Taylor of Warrington. The Chairman said they were not disposed to convict her of an offence committed eight years ago but hoped it would 1e a warn- ing to her. The case would be dismissed. SCHOOL CASES.—Mr.G. . BUCKLEY-ST. (WARRINGTON) V. WIDNES RED ROSE.— This match was played at Widnes on Saturday, and ended in an easy win for the Red Rosm. aiker bowled well for the losers, as also did R. and W. Hague for the winners. Both sides fielded well. Result :—Buckley-st., 15; Red Rose, 29.

BUCKLEY-STREET (WARRINGTON) T. WIDNES RED ROSE. This match was played at Widnes on Saturday, and ended in an easy win for the Red Rose. Baker bowled well for the losers, as also did rt. and W. Hague for the winners. Both sides fielded well. Result :—Buckley- street, 15 ; Red Rose, 29. WARRINGTON V. WERNETEL—Unfortunately for this im- portant fixture, Warrington were unable to send their best team to Werneth on Saturday, Messrs. Crosfield being absent owing to a family bereavement. The weather was cold for the summer sport, but the atten- dance was nevertheless good. Werneth won the toss, and taking up the batting first the venture closing for 73 runs. Bates bowled splendidly, maintaining a capi- tal length throughout, whilst, the way he tricked the three players when they returned the balls to him stam- ped him as being above the ordinary run of professienal cricketers. Warrington started their essay with G. Clarke and Bates. A disastrous start was made, for Bates was given out in trying a second run, the decision of the home umpire being received witt groans by the impartial spectators. Warrington's ill-luck did not end here, for with the total at seven and eight two more wickets fell, Wordsworth being taken smartly at the wicket, whilst Clarke was bowled with one of Hall's famous leg-breaks. Callon followed, with the score at 17 he failed to secure proper hold of one from Hall, and Hilton took the catch at short leg. .With the junior pro- fessional, Clark, joining the Warrington skipper, a deter- mined resistance was offered, and boundaries .were rapidly notched, so that at 37 Bedford took the ball from Hodgkinson. It was left to Hall to effect a separation, and at 45 a good ball from the Werneth professional sent Smith back, the outgoing batsman having shown excel- lent form for his score of 19. Clark continued to bat nicely, but though E. Connor aided him a little, no as- sistance was foithcoming from the others, who failed completely before the deliveries of Hall. Bedford met with success at last, for Clark was too late with an extra fast one, and at the close Warrington were in a minority by 16 runs. The fielding of Warrington was excellent throughout, whilst the wicket-keeping of Clark was of the usual high order. Score and bowling analysis:—

ST. MARY'S v. LATCHFORD.

FINAL TIE.

Owing to the St. Mary's and Latchford teams felling Lo come to any definirn conclusion on April 30:h, in the final tie for the Warrington JuniQr Rugby C-p pre- sented by Mr. Percy Davies, they met- to contiraie the struggle on Tuesday night. As it is the " close" season at present, no "gate " money was charged.and as might be expected there was a large number of spectators to witness the game. Mr.J. Smith of Widnes attain acted as referee. The game was commenced by Mr. Percy Davies kicking off towards the pavilion goal,and Latchford who had a slight wind behind them at once pressed. Chiefly through the instrumentality of Houghton and Hughes, the St. Mary's 25 was passed and Latchford looked like scorers, hut Hampson and Burton kept them at bay. Off side tactics by Latchford further relieved St.Mary'S, hut Houghton again returned to the attack, and the Latchford men rushed the hall over the line, and Bur- ton rushing up kicked it dead. Burton restarted, but Latchford kept up the attack and a series of scrimmages ensued on the St. Mary's line. Up to this period, Latch- ford had had decidedly the upper hand, the St. Mary's team being scarcely ever as far as half way, lout they now woke up, and a grand dribble by Burton, three- quarters the length of the field .ought to have ended in something tang,ibla resulting but he was milled up just in time. Immediately afterwards, Warburton was pulled up on the line after a desperate struggle on that ! player's part to ground. Similar bad luck followed 1 some good play of Bennett. Latchford now had a short look in, and Roughley saved well with the opposing for- wards upon him. He was soon afterwards cnmpelled to concede a. minor. An exciting struggle ensued in the Latchford 25 which resulted in Burton getting over at the corner. He was unsuccessful however in improving ! the point. Within two minutes after this scare, Swift and Raybold each get over but the points were dis- allowed. After desultory plaY, Latchford by a sudden attack got over in a favourable position, but Carter could not increase the score. Another minor quickly fell to Latchford, and half time then arrival wirh the contestants having a try each to their credit.. After the interval, capital passing between the St. Mary's backs gave that team a decided advantage, and Bennett securing the ball passed it to Thomas when near the line, and that player successfully grounded the ball. Burt,n was aeain unsuccessful with the place. Chielly owing to a !null by Chapman, Burton got over the line again, but Swift's attempt at the goal was a failure. Latchford now made a spurt and Roughley waa called upon and not found wanting. St. Mary's returned to the attack and a too strong kick by Burton caused an almost certain try to be lost, only a minor resulting. He retrieved this error a short time afterwards however by getting over and scoring a very meritorious try,llelsby's kick at goal result- ing like all the rest. hayfield was the next scorer for St. Mary's. Although they hail suffered so many reverses, Latchford played well, and the St. Mary's supporters had great cause for anxiety, but were relieved when Roughley saved under the goal posts, and sent them back. . There was no further pay of any interest, and a good game ended with the score of St. Mary's five tries. Latchford one try. Immediately after the conclusion of the game, the cup and medals were presented to Berry, captain of the St. Mary's team. by Mr. Percy Davies, who congratulated him upon the success of his team. Three cheers were then heartily given to the winners, and Mr. Ashcroft replied on behalf of the St. Mary's thanking Mr. Davies for his generosity in giving the cue to be contested for and expressed the de- termination of the St. Mary's team to keep it now that they had got it. The cup was afterwards taken in triumph to the headquarters of the St. Mary's, in Bank Quay, where it will remain until next season.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VAGARIES OF THE LAW.

In Central Switzerland the opponents of captial pun- ishment have raised a curious point of law in support of their movement. The affair arises out of the conviction of a monster named Abegg, who recently murdered his daughter. The unfortunate girl was a domestic servant, and the father a ne'er-do-weel and a drunkard who was perpetually extorting money from her. One day he met her at a little town en the Lake of Zurich, where she was employed, and because she refused to help him any more, he stabbed her, first in the neck, and then in the chest, the knife penetrating her heart. The murderer then coolly took a silk handkerchief and a basket from his victim, and went and changed them at a drinking shop fora cup of coffee and a glass of brandy. He was sentenced at the Assize Court at :+chwytz to he guillo- tined, whereupon a local society for the abolition of the punishment of death appealed against the sentence on the ground that, according to law, condemned criminalg must he ioaheaded by the sword. The Court of Appeal decided that their view of the law was correct, and this causes a dead-lock, as no man can he found in Switzer- land to carry out th 3 sentence of the law in the manner required.

DEATH AND DISEASE AT WAR 13 IN GTO N.

THE MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. John H. Gornall, medical officer of health for the borou2h, has just issued his annual report for the year 1893. THE YEAR'S DEATHS.

It statea that the number of deaths recorded in the borough during the ) ear 1893 was 1,391, of which 783 were males and 603 females ; 1,297 were registered in the Warrington and 94 in the Latchford Registration District. Of this number 40 took place at the Fever Hospital, 13 at the Hope Hospital for small-pox, 18 at the Infirmary, and 96 at the Workhouse. In estimating deathrate, 23 deaths must be deducted of persons belonging to other districts. The death rate for the year was 24.8 per 1,000 of the population, the highest recorded since 1879. This is partially due to the continuance of small-pox in the first six months of the year, but mainly to the fearful mortality from diarrhcea amongst young children in the latter six months of the year. The annual mortality is 4.6 higher than the average of large urban districts, and 56 higher than the rate for England as a whole, and 7.4 higher than the rate for rural districts. The death rate in the several Wards of the Borough were as follows :—Town Hall, estimated population, midsummer, 1893. 6.374, death rate ner 1.000 26.3 ; Nl'llite:h.oss 6.290, 29.2 ; Bewsev, 5.734, 245 ; ....John's 9,152. 30.8 ; Fairfield, 5,176, 21.5 ; Orford 5,538, 26 0 ; Howley 6,698, 26 7; St. Austin's 4,086, 19 5 ; Latchford, 6,042, 172. It should be ex- plained that the nine deaths which occurred at the Workhouse being credited to Whitecross increases the rate of that Ward abnormally.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
INFANTILE MORTALITY.

Of the 783 deaths registered in 1893 in children under five years, 458 were infants under 1 year. Calculated by the proportion of deaths under 1 year of age to the births registered, the infant mortality in Warrington, for the year 1893, was equal 208 per 1;000. This exces- sive mortality, though hpparently due to the large number of deaths from zymotic diarrhoea, 128 being ascribed to this cause of children under 1 year of age, is indirectly due to the inability on the part of the mothers to supply their children with the natural food. Com- bined with this we have the insanitary conditions under which the working classes live ; the improper and unwholesome food given, as well as personal uncleanli- ness and neglect.

DEATHS FROM ZYMOTIC DISEASES.

The zymotic death rate reached the very high point of 6.8, the hiv.hest since 1882, due to the excessive mortal- ity, from diarrhoea, this alone accounting for 3-6 per 1,000.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
SCARLET FEVER.

This disease was again prevalent. The total number notified was 364 ; of thes«. 43 were fatal—equal to 12 per cent, of the cases, and 0.78 per 1,0)0 of the population. Of this number 289 were removed to the hospital, 28 proving. fatal,or 10 per cent. ; of the remaind2 r, 66, 15 died, or 26 per cent.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
ENTERIC FEVF.R.

Forty-one eases of this disease were notified; 25 of these were removed to the hospital, one was treated at the Workhouse, one out of town, and the remainder at their own homes. There were 17 deaths, 11 in the fever hospitalloand the remainder at their own homes. I have investigated the sanitary surroundings of all the eases, and am satisfied that neither the milk nor water supply are responsible.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
DEFECTIVE DRAINAGE.

Having referred to several serious cases of defective drainage, the report continued : In previous years I have referred to the offensive gases given off by the sewers, and specially in those streets which are cul-de- sacs, with the sewers commehcing at the top. This was well marked in 1887, when numerous cases occurred in Howard-st., Wakefield-st., sod Pierpoint-st., all streets the sewers of which are dead-ends. The inhalation of sewer gas from the street ventilators atapears also to be closely connected with the existence of enteric feyer,for we find in manyof the cases thventilat or in close prox- imity to the house in which the patient lived, and a noticeable fact is that a certain number of the cases oc- curred in the higher portions of the town, for the tendency of the Air-currents is to pass in the opposite d•rection ti the flow of sewa,4e, sewer gas rising from the low to the higher level. Thiir, points to the importance of securing to the town as perfect a system of sewerage as possible, and I rectal-ma:lent] with this object in view that the Borough Surt'eyor should be c.insulted on the following points :—(1.) The thorough cleansing of the sewers. (2. ) On the necessity of improving the ventila- tion of the sewers, especially in the streets with dead ends, and in crowded districts. (3.) That in addition to the occasional flushing of the sewers which at pri:sent is only carried out, that some form of automatic flushing in the streets which are cztl. de-sacs be entertained.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
CAUSES or DIARRHOEA.

The year was essential!y a diarrhoea one, with little rain of high temperature. In 53 of the cases there was uncleanness of the fc,iling apparatus, and out of the total 199 deaths, in 117 there was defective drainage and other insanitary conditions.

THE RIVER 'MERSEY AND THE OLD BED OF THE RIVER

===== 0002601_18940512_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
CUT OFF BY THE SHIP CANAL.

During the dry, hot weansr of last year, the river ho- came very low, and in Latchcord especially gave off a most vile stench. I fear, that in the event of similar meteorological conditions during the approaching sum- mer, we have only to expect an aggravated form of the same nuisance. for the drain upon the water of the stream by the Ship Canal will furnish yet another con- tributory cause. It was pointed out in the report for 1889 that several cases of Enteric Fever had occurred in the Knutsfard-road, and of the associated in- sanitary conditions, the most marked was the existence of offensive emanations coming from the river, and penetrating the houses concerned. The evil of such nuisa flees cannot be tom strongly insisted upon. The making of the Ship Canal may also he productive of a danger in another way. If we suffer from the proximity of a running stream n in dry weather, what must we expect from a huge cesspool— for this is what, the old portion of the river along Wil- derspool has practically become in consequence of the Mersey diversion. Culverts have been, I believe, con- structed to ensure some renewal of the water at each tide, but are really insufficient for the purpose ; and, moreover, cause the water at each end to be stirred up with th emanation of poisonous gases. I desire to call your attention to these matters, with a view to some tmasure for the protection of • the inhabitants of the town.

THE CHIEF SANITARY REQUIREMENTS OF THE BOROUGH. Under this had were classified : (1) Dealing with the Insanitary Area, reported on in 1892, (2) carrying out in a more enpr,r,tic manner the housing of the working classes act, 1890, so as to improve, wherever practicable the ventilation of houses,and making back to• back tene- ments into through houses. (3) paving and making of the unmade streets, back passages, courts. yards, &c., Sz?,. (4) the improvement of the sewerage. (5) Increased hospital accommodation.

T RADES DEHONSTRA.TION AT WIDNES.

The second annual demonstration promoted by Vie Widnes and District Trades Council was held on Saturday. The several societies mustered on the promenade wl- ere the procession was formed,under the marshalling of Mr. J. Byrne. Shortly after three o'clock the procession proceeded through the principal parts of the town in the following order: —The carpenters, headed by the Weston Brass Band; coopers, with the St. Mary's Band • moulders ac- companied by the Mersey Mission Life Band; bricklayers led by the Widnes Jubilee; plumbers, headed by the Widnes Subscription Band ; and the bakers. Afterwards an open air meeting was held on the yarant land near St. Paul's Church, there being about 2,000 persons present, Alderman T. Snape M.P., pre- sided and he was supported by Councillor Shaftoe J.P., of Bradford ; Mr. G. D. Kelly, secretary Manchester and and S Ilford Trades Council ; Mr. Sheppard. president; and Mr. G .H.Cherry.Secretary of the Operative Plumbers. The Chairman said they had arrived at a most eventful period in the history of labour in this country. They had now in Parliament a greater number of representa- tives (men who had taken part in manual labour them- selves) than ever they had. He then referred to the action of the House of Lords in reference to the Em- ployers Liability Bill. Personally he was glad the Government had withdrawn the Bill rather than accept the amendment of the House of Lords. Referring to Messrs. Mather and Platt of Salford, he said they bad set a good example by introducing an eight hours' day into their workshops, and after a trial of twelve months had expressed their satis- faction with the results.—Councillor Shaftoe moved and Mr. Cherry seconded, the following resolution : ' That:this meeting of trades unionists rejoices in the continued progress of the labour movement, as indicated by tbe ad iption of the eight hours' day iu Government workshops and by other private employers of labour, and cordially thanks her Majesty's Government for their various administrative acts tending to the advantage of organised labour."—The Chairman in putting the resolu- tion, referred to the dispute amongst the bricklayers in Widnes. He was not in favour of strikes, nor did he think trades unionists believed in them. If he thought trades unions existed to support strikes he would not have been there at that meeting. He was in favour of a board of concilia'ion for the settlement of disputes. He hoped that something of the kind would be formed in Widnes so that the dispute might be brought to a satis- factory issue.—The resolution was then put by him and carried unanimously.—Mr. G. D. Kelly moved the fol- lowing resolution :—" That this meeting emphatically condemns the action of the House of Lords in introduc- ing a clause allowing contracting-out of the Employers' Liability Bill, and while approving the action of the Government in withdrawing the bill, trusts they will re- introduce it at an early date."—Mr. Sheppard seconded, and on the resolution being put to the meeting was carried unanimously.—A vote of thanks to the Chairman closed the proceedings.

THE WINDING UP OF THE RUNCORN WATERWORKS COMPANY,

On Monday afternoon an extraordinary general meeting of the Runcorn, Weston, and Halton Waterworks Company was held in the Public Hall, Runcorn, for the purpose of declaring a dividend for the quarter ended the 307,11 Sept., 1593 ; passing the accounts on the winding up of the Company, and determining the amount to he paid to the officers of the Company under section 11, suh-section three of the Runcorn Commissioners Act, 1893, and in what propor- tions. Mr. John Brundrit, J chairman of directors, presided, and there were ako present : Messrs. John Simpson, William Whittaker, William Hayes (directors) ; Mrs. Shaw, and Messrs. T. D. Timmins, R. Foulkes, James Collier, W. J. Clarke, T. Wril4tit, J. Ashton (solicitor). W. F. Salkeld (secretary), and James L. Palma (clerk).

DIRECTORS REPORT AND FINANCIAL STATEMENT.

The Directors reported that the revenue account showed a balance of £l2BB 7s. 11. out of which they re- commended a dividend of 5 per cent per annum free from income tax for the quarter ended September 1893. The capital account showed the purchase money of the undert iking from the RAncorti Inprovernent Commis- sioners to be £67,500, and the Bank interest £l4 16s. 9d. The balance of the account for the half year ended June 3Jth, 1893, was 16s. id. ; the mortgage and bond debts, £2,400 the holders of shares in the capital of the company entitled to 'a preferential dividend, being a sum equal to 28 years' purchase of the amount of such dividend, £24,437; ordinary sharelinider3 ret account, £37,500; balance of new boiler acccunt, 7s. Bd.; Parliatnen- tory expenses and law costs to the 31st March, 1894, £BB7 95.; accountants charges in connection with the sale, £lO5 ; bankers commission on division of capital, £5O ; amount transferred from revenue account to capi- tal account, under the title of depreciation fand in the years 1879-80, £l,OOO ; balance, £903 43.; total, £67,514 163. 9d. The revenue account showed a balance from the half-year ending June 30th, 1893, after dividends amounting to £750 had been paid, £451 17s. 4e1.; water rates, rents, fittings, and meter rents realised £2.007 14s. 11d.; stock taken by the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners, as per valuation, £503 134.; amount transferred from revenue to capital account, under the title of depreciation fund, in the year 1879,80, less stock credited to revenue account in 1879 to 1893, £479 25.11 d. charged in revenue account for extension in mains in 1880, £97 .193. 7d.• total, £3,51-4 lie. 4d. The credit side showed that after meeting all expenses there re- mained a balance of £1,288 7s. id. Mr. Salkeld pre- sented the auditors certificate which stated that every thing had been found correct upon examination.—The Chairman in proposing the adoption of the accounts and that the dividend mentioned be paid remarked that that was the last meeting of the company at which they would declare a dividend. He felt a sort of lingering regret in parting with 'an Ins'itution in which he had been so greatly interested. Like many other things it had been troublesome in its infancy, and they had great trouble to get it into working order in ordnr that divi- dends might be paid. After much carefulness and watchfulness the company had become prosperous, and had sold the works now to the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners. He thougt.t the Commissioners had made a good purchase for the town and would find it to their advantige,—Mr. Timmins seconded and the leport and accounts were unanimously adopted.

===== 0002601_18940512_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMPANY'S OFFICIALS.

The Chairman ob3erved that during the passing of the Runcorn Commissioners Act, 1893. a clause was inserted to the effect that the company's officials might be remu- nerated by a fit amount for being deprived of their income. At the time of transfer they had had to give notice to their officials that their services would no longer be required. Some were taken over lw the Commission- ers and some were not, and the directors had given the matter of remuneration their careful consideration. Their recommendation which would require the shareholders approval, was that the following sums be paid :Mr. Salkeld. £l5O ; Mr, James L. Palin, £4O ; Mr. Walter Jones, £4O; and to the office boy £5. The auditors had been appointed yearly but the directors thought that as they had had considerable trouble lately £5 should be given to each of them.—Mr. Hayes seconded the propo- sition saving that all had been very efficient servants.— The recommendation was adooteci.—Mr. Foulkes and Mr. Collier both expressed their thanks for the consider- ation they had received as auditors from the share- holders.

THE REMUNERATION OF THE DIRECTORS.

Mr. Foulkes asked Mr. Ashton if the present was the proper time to speak of remunerating the directors who had worked so hard for such a number of vears.—Mr. Ashton replied in the affirmative.—Mr. Collier proposed that the sum of £7OO be given to the directors.—The proposition was seconded by Mr. Timmins with a slight alteration, viz., that the amount be £750. —The Chair- man said that the proposition was not one which could be passed at that meeting as no notice had been given of such a matter.—Mr. Collier then moved that a meeting be convened for the purpose of considering the matter on the 28th May, at 3 p.m. He accepted Mr. Timmins alteration, namely, that the sum should be £750 and he would have great pleasure in proposing that amount at the next theeting.—Mr. Timmins seconded, and the motion was carried.—Mr. Salkeld having returned thanks to the directors and shareholders for their kind- ness, a vote of thanks was accordea the Chairman which terminated the proceedings.

John Drury, a well-known figure in Warrington, and for a long number of years associated with the Temperance cause in the town celebrated his seven- tieth birthday laq week. A tea aril meeting was held in the Workingmen's Gospel Tern ,eranee Mis- sion Hall in the evening when the workers in con- nection with the mission and a few invited friends were present. An interesting feature of he even- ing's proceedings was the presentations which were made to several persons connec,ed with the mission. Mr. Drury who occupied the chair pre- sented on behalf of the committee a beautifully engraved glass jug to Mr. and Mrs. Kgginpczton, and also to Mr. and Mrs. Horrocks as an acknowlegment of their services in temperance work. Mr. -Rigby (secre- tary) presented Mr. Drury with some glass-ware and remarked that it was some thirty years since Mr. Drury signed the temperance pledge. He had pleasure in making him that present in commemoration of his seventieth birthday. Mr. If. Storey then read a list of siA)scribers, numbering 50, of friends and members of the Gospel Temperance Hall, who felt " that it was fitting that an acknowledgment of Mr. Drury's devoted services to the cause they had at heart should be made on the occasion of his 70th birthday." The total amount subscribed was £lO Ns. and £lO 103. 6d. of this amount was placed in a purse bearing the inscription " Presented to John Drury, on his 70th, birthday, May 4th, 1894." Mr. Storey said he had VW, joy in handing the purse to Mr. Drury, and he read a sonnet of his own composition on the event. Mr Drury, in acknowledging the gift, said he was unable to dad words to express his thankfulness to the subscribers. He had done cc hat he could to reform the poor drunkard. He didn't think that he had so many friends in Warring- ton who thought of him and his efforts in the cause of temperance, but he was determined as lone as he was spared to still go on in the work.—Mr. Wm. Roberts read a letter from Miss Bessie Dutton, president .f the Tem- perance, Mission, regretting her inability to be present at the meeting, and sating that she would have delighted in co-operating personally in their work of doing honour to so high-minded, indefatigable, and devoted a worker in their glorious old cause as Mr. Drury. Mr. Roberts said he had known Mr. and Mrs. Drary almost as 'soon as they came to Warrington, and he looked upon him as a very valuable servant in the cause of temperance. It was often said of their temper- ance men that they were extreme men and some had been wicked enough to say that they were not Christian men. He could say that of all the men he had known in connection with the temperence movement they had been Christian men. They were told also that temper- ance men were the most intemperate in their speeches. But they believed that they could not speak too strongly on the evils of intemperance. There were some in that room who knew too well what terrible results accrued from the use of intoxicating liquors. Mr. Drury was one of those who had laboured in the days when tem- perance was unpopular, and now that they saw the dawn of a brighter and better day they revered such men for their past labours.—Mr. 'Daft referred to some recent criticisms on temperance workers, and spoke of the importance of women workers in the temperance cause. —Messrs. Hnrrocks, Tanner, Stringer hnd S. Blythe also spoke, referring to Mr. Drury's work in cnnection with temperance.

THE SCORERS. The scoring has been consistently good all round, and the backs naturally claim the leading positions. Potts leads the way with a dropped goal, a penalty vat and nine tries, closely followed by James Bate and Carey with nine tries each. Evans stands by himself with eight tries, whilst John Massey, John Bate, and J. Tay- lor are all bracketed together with six tries ( aCh ; F. Barber, J. Berry, J. Jolley, and G. Dakin have all suc- ceeded in crossing the line on five occasions, whilst that veteran player,Fair. Turner has three tries to his credit, and J. Houghton and F. Heesom two tries each. Single points have been scored by F.Broady,J.Higham,E.Bailey, W. Handles, S. Buckley, W. Nevins, J. Holmes, J. T. Thorniley, W. Burton, and T. Donohue. Goals fmm tries have been placed by Potts 10, neesom and Evans 7 each. Fair. Turner 3, and Taylor and Phillips 1 each. Fair. Turner dropped a goal from a fair catch against Broughton Rangers away. SUPPER AT TIIE NORTON ARMS.

Thursday last week Mr. Percy Davies. president of the Warrington Football Club, entertained the players of the first and A. teams, and also the offi- cials of the club in the Norton Arms Hotel,the club's headquarters. Amongst those present were Mr. Thomas Morris, Mr. William Pierpoint, J.A., and Alder- man Flutchinscn, The loyal and patriotic toasts were duly honoured,whilst the toast of the evening, " Succsss to the Warrington Foothill Club," prop•seA by Mr. Morris, was received with enthusiastic cheers. In replying, the president referred to the honourable position the native players of Warrington held in the Lancashire League. Though standing third in the League, their position was by far the most honourable, because every metnbei of the first fifteen and also of the A team was a Warrington man born and bred, whereas in Oldham,with its population of 100,000, not more than four,if ther, were so many, were genuine Oldham men ; the others were from here, there, and everywhere ; and Swinton,though representing the finest players of Manchester aryl Salford, with a population of half a million, were also a nondescript club as regards membership. Warrington had, therefore, great reason to be proud of her native-born players. Mr. Davies presented a silver jug to Mr. John Bate, captain of the first team, and also a silver cup to Mr. Morris, as president or the cycling club, as a prize to he competed for at the foiticoming cycling sports. Messrs. Anderton. Parr, Mounfi,Ad, Taylor, Perry, Forshaw,and Haynes contributed to the musical proceedings of the evening, Mr. Thomas Lane, o,rganist, of Walton Church, being the accompanist.

WARRINGTON TECHNICAL INSTITUTE.

Wednesday last weekbeing the concluding evening I ot the session for the class on building construction held at the School of Art, opportunity was taken for a brief but interesting ceremony, the occasion being the presentation of a prize awarded by Mr. H. J. Angel, A.U.1.8.A., the lecturer, to Mr. W. J. Ball for the best work done during the session and most regular attendance. The presentation was made by Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., the ohairtm-in of the Committee. In a few remarks,Mr. Monks spoke words of congratu- lation and encouragement to the students. Referring to Lila own early experience, he told how, with few and primitive in,truments, he worked geometrical problems on the floor of his workshop, 'lnd thus by industry, per- severance and strict adhtsion to the path of duty, he built the (Froat business which he now enjoys, Con- trasting Coe present advantages obtained at the Institute with those of his own early life, he showed in how much better a position the present eay students were situated in being equipned for the battle of life, and he looked forward with great expectation to the future of the students. One may form an idea of he interest taken in the class by the students when it is mentioned that three students have never missed ail attVrtanre, one has only been b,zent once, and several others come very near those fl:2llret, NAide t ni drawings and other wet k on the whole have been excellent ; great asßista.nce being derived from the very complete set of building models possessed by the Institute.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Sanitary Authority held on Friday, the Rev. G. F. Roberts presiding, Mr. R. _Heaton proposed that the Authority should appoint a deputation to attend the conference of Urban and Rural Sanitary Authorities to be held in London, and the pro- position was seconded by Mr. W. Houghton. Mr. R. Ownti said : "I object to the proposition on these grounds. They had outside the Warrington boun- dary a great nuisance in the shape of the Warrington Manure Works. It had devastated acres of land in the neighbourhood—or at least,ruisied their value for build- ing purposes. Through this the neighbourhood is denied the privileges of any other suburb in Warrington. The nuisance is an abominable one, and the smells which emanate from the works are nauseous and repulsive to the senses. At on" time gentlemen did reside in the neighbourhood, but they have been driven away. The nuisance 11,d continued Icr years, and yet that Autho- rity had never done one stroke of work to prevent it. Under these circumstances, he contended that it was farcical for them to send a deputation to study Sanitary Science when the Authority would not move to abate a nuisance which could easily be prevented." Mr. JOLLEY : I support Mr. Owen's objections. Let the Authority read the reports of the conference in the London newspapers if they wish to do so. The CHAIRMAN : I think the Authority has done whir t it cm in the matter, Mr. Owen. Mr. OWEN : Done what it can I deny it. Why the old system of burning the vegetable matter was far better than the present one. It is simply a matter of cooking the excreta and letting the noisome odours at large. It is more than humanity can bear. Mr. W. HOUGHTON : I think your argument would he better in support of the motion. Nuisances of all kinds can wily be cleared away by the aid of a knowledge of sanitary science.

Mr. 0 WENT : Then let a deputation requesting the Warrington Corporation to send someone to the confer- ence so that they, might beassisted to a knowledge of how to prevent the nuisance. Mr. It. HEATON : They are going to send. I might mention that the destructor of which Mr. Owen com- plains is a similar one to those used in the manure works of other large towns where no complaints are made about nuisances. Why not quarter of a mile from Man- chester Central Station there is one in a works where more manure is manufactured in a day than there is in a week in Warrington. _ _ . _

Mr. J. C. SUTTON said that there was no deubt the nuisance aluded to by Mr Owen was a very s.n.ious one and it required abating. Hitherto all the authority had done was to waste w-)rds and correspondence on the Warrington Corporation. He thought the time had, ar- rived now when nay should spend something on the subject, and enforce their points. Mr. OWEN'S objections however, he thought were only arguments in favour of the motion for it was only by getting a knowledge of the way other people dealt with these matters that they could hope to be able to suggest remedies.

Mr. OWEN : It isn't any use. The Corporation will not be dictated to by us. Thr motion was then put to the vote and was carriepl and the Chairman (the Rev. F. G. Roberts) the Clerk (Mr. S. Sutton) and Mr. R. Heaton were appointed as a deputation,

TB.E NEW PUBLIC HALL FOR WARRINGTON.

The following information about the new Public Hall which is now being erected by Mr. J. Charlton Parr is given us by the architect Mr. William Owen, of Cairo-street, by Mr, Parr's permission. The hall will stand on the South side of Palmyra Square, nearly adjoining the Gymnasium, parallel to the street and set back a yard and a half from the frontage line, so as to give a wider footpath for the public using it. The hall will be 108 feet by 64-? feet, inside, exclusive of an organ chamber 30ft. by 16 feet. The bcdy of the Hall on the ground floor will be 90ft.by 48-ft which is surrounded on its four sides with corriders 6,7, and 10 feet wide respe,- tively. Fronting the street will be an entrance hall 50 feet by 161 feet, with ladies' cloakroom, 20 feet by 16?; feet ; ladies' retiring room, 18 feet by 17 feet ; and lavatory accommodation at one end. At the other erad gentleman's cloakroom 181 feet by 15i feet, with lavatory accommodation. Access will be gained from the entrance hall into the inner corridor which surrounding the seat space on the ground floor, will give admission by eight doors to almost any portion of the hall without interruption. On the ground floor at the east end there will be iepar- ate rooms for gentlemen and lady speakers or per- formers with lavatory accommodation. On the gallery floor at the west end there will be a front gallery 121 feet wide and a back gallery 19 feet wide, whilst at the sides of the hall there will be galleries 13 teet wide. These side galleries by covering the corridors on the ground floor, project only over the bodv:of the hall about 5 feet. Entrance to the gallery is obtained at the west end by a stone staircase with steps 6, feet wide. The gallery has three exit doors two of which are staircases perfectly direct with two landings on each to break any fall. From the ground floor there are eight means of exit. The object of the corridor being continued round the body of the hall is to prevent draught when people are entering the hall. In the basement there is a heati:-.g chamber and fair sized kitchen. The platform has a width of 15 feet with a rising tier of steps behind for the accommodation of a choir. The platform is reached from the private corri- dor by stairs on either side. The organ chamber is immediately behind it and on the gallery level nere is a retiring room for lady choristers 181 -feet by 171 feet with lavatory accommodation in addition. By the removal of partitions between the ladies' cloakroom and the entrance hall and the gentlemen's cloakroom and entrance hall the entrance can be made 85 feet long and can be used as a refreshment room in connection with the hall. The clear height of the hall inside is 45 feet. Comfortably seated, inclusive of platform, the hall will accommodate about 1,500 people, but for political and other crowded meetings the hall will accommodate with fair comfort 2,000 people. The building has been designed by Mr. Owen, of Cairo-street Chambers, and the contract has been let to Mr. 11. W. Collin who is already engaged upon the work.
