tetttrO to tin Otittor.

THE RECTOR AND THE DRINK TRAFFIC. Sir,—The recent utterances of the Rector at the Town Hall meeting in support of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission, have given intense pain to a large number of people who are in complete sympathy with the objects of this Society. The Rector has a perfect right to his Gpinions no matter how extraordinary some of them mar appear to many, hut he has no right whatever at a meeting called for a specific object to introduce matters ' entirely irrelevant to the purpose for which the meeting was called together. If those responsible for the meeting permit this kind of thing to occur again they must be prepared for opposition and probably withdrawals of a serious nature from the subseription list. With reference to the Rector's blessing upon the trade, for this is what his speech amounts to, I would ask him to visit the public- houses in Warrington any Saturday night in order to witness how these " God gifts of the Almighty " are being abused by large masses of the people. I cannot believe that after witnessing the harrowing and distress- ing scenes which nightly occur in many public-houses in Warrington our Rector would continue to hold the opinions he now gives utterance to. It may be a surprise for the Rector to know that upcn a recent temperance Sunday when a sermon was preached by himself ,bearing upon this subject, a number of worshippers at the close of the service assembled else- where and drank the Rector's health, If this is the kos ult of temperance sermons at the Parish Church, I would respectfully suggest to the Rector that they be quietly dropped altogether.—Yours, TEMPERANCE.

Measter Heditor,—Whilst readin' ower yore excellunt mar last wick I wus strucken wi'th' speech oth Rector. flee seames a chap ut understons human natur for he trys praise thoo's ut supply& him wi plenty o' brass; he mun keep thick wi' brewers as thean plenty o' money and are glad to part wi' it so lung as their trade is kept respect- able. I think 'it Rector wad o' bin mute in his place if he'd'been on th' side o'th oppressed instead o'th oppres- sor, and he wod soon o' bein' independent o'th brewers if folks wus sober as thieved hey more money to give when they went to church. Mr. Heditor, I think drink is wrung, becose when I was a lad we hod a miserable whoarn thro' nay father drinking Greenall's knock-um- deaun. Eaur hease on Sunday was like a bedlam, full o' drunken men. Drinking must be wrung as it robs us childer o' cloas and comfort and peace. It turns us out o' door at neet to walk streets, and I am one o' those et think we can't be to strick to rid o'lh evil o'th drink traffic. Mr. Rector, Christ never did give intoxicating drink, becose it talcs so inony days to ferment and he wodn't break Sabbath bi brewing. Mr. Rector, I'm one ut beleove what that chap ut lived above a 100 years ago, John Wesley, woe nearer truth when he said about those who sold drink: "All who sell in the common ways that will buy are poisoners , general ; they murder his Majesty's subjects wholesale ; Neither do they pity or spare. They drive them to Hell like sheep. Who would envy their large estates and sumptuous palaces. A curse is in the midst (41 them. The curse of God cleaves to the stones, the timber, the furniture of them, a curse is in their gardens. their walks, their groves—a fire that burns to the nethermost Hell. Blood, blood, is their foundations—the floor, the wall, the roof, are stained with blood." I hope, Mr. Rector, yo'll come on reight side, and safe side, and best side,and wisest, and on Christ's sicle,and yo'll be happier and safer anti do more good.—Yours truly, ONE WHO HAS SUFFERED BUT NEVER TASTED.

-- Sir,—lo his speech last week in connection with the Prison Gate Mission the Rector referred to certain critic- isms which had been passed upon the Church of England in Warrington,on account of its receiving large benefactions from the local brewers. I believe his remarks had reference to certain statements made by me at a recent meeting of the Debating Society. I said 'that the Church of England in . Warrington was subsid- ized by the drink interest in the town and explained this by saying that owing to its receiving large contributions from Sir Gilbert Greenall and other brewers, the Church could not help being paralized in any efforts it might desire to make to deal with thoie great evils which were the direct outcome of the brewer's trade. This was on the occasion of a debate on the "Social Questions Union." The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, who is a member of the Debating Society, was present, and he promptly and indignantly repudiated the charge, describing it as a disgraceful one. He went on to ask what had Holy Trinity Church and schools received from the drink interest of the town ? " Not a farthing," lie replied. How much of his salary was eentributed by the drink interest ? "Not a farthing." Well, I did not know at the time, but have been told since that Mr. Carleton received £1,500 from Sir Gilbert towards St. Luke's Mission Church at Sankey Bridges. But Mr. Carleton did not say a word about this. Neither did he tell us anything about St. James', or St. Petee's. He simply met my statement by a flat denial, and described it as a , aeriou3 and disgraceful charge. The Rector, however, in his speech last week, said he did not for a moment deny the truth of this statement. "He was only too thankful to acknowledge publicly and gratefully the help he had received from Sir Gilbert Greenall and Sir Andrew Walker." The Rector's admis- sion possesses at least the merit of candour, which is certainly more than can be said for Mr. Carleton. But what can be said for the position taken up by the Rector? He acknowledges that chink is the great cause of the crime and misery which we see around us, and yet • he does not think that the drink traffic is wrong,and so is not ashamed to receive gifts from the brewers. I confess lamat a loss to understand the Rector. See- ing the appalling extent of the crime and misery which are directly due to drink, I do not see how the drink traffic can help being very wrong indeed. Judge after judge haetsld us that nineteen-twentieths- of . all the crime in the country is directly or indirectly due to drink; that but for drink there would be practically little or no crime. Ministers of religion and social re- formers have told us again and again that the seething mass of misery, of destitution, of vice and immorality which are such prominent and painful factors in our present day civilization are directly due to drink. The drinking habits of the people are the despair of all who desire their moral, religious, and material elevation. These habits stand as an almost insuperable barrier in the •way of all progress and development. And yet the trade ,which engenders and fosters these habits is not wrong! Many of your readers will be surprised and pained to "be told by the Rector that the Bible from beginning to end allowed the moderate use of intoxicants, and that '" Our Saviour" gave it in large quentities, not for reli- gious purposes hut for enjoyment. I hope the Enter will pardon me if I say that the whole tenor of his remarks is very much like asking the brewers for more. He goes on to suggest certain reforms in the drink traffic, but as you pertinently ask in your article last week, "Are the clergy of the established church conspicuous in their support of the politicians who are in favour of re- stricting the drink traffic?" Decidedly, no. and in Warrington are the clergy doing anything to bring about a healthier state of affairs? Have they appeared at the police court during the Brewster Sessions to endeavour to bring about any reduction of the licenses which they admit to be superfluous? Have they raised a voice or a finger in protest against the attempt to impose a ring of brewers and publicans upon the town as Councillors? Hoe, can they do anything of this nature with any strenu- ous, whole-heartedness when so large a share of their -support comes frolin the very source which is directly answerable for the terrible evils we all deplore ?—Yours ruly, JOHN GANDY.

T.Y.H. AND SOCIALISM.

Sir,—A very little of your space will suffice for what I have to say in answer to Mr. Howcroft. Sweetness and light could not possibly he evolved nut of any discussion between such a person and myself, therefore the briefer the better. As a " Free LAnce " correepoudent to the columns of the"Examiner,"l have during the past twenty years, I confess, had much to say anent most current questions, and I have, invariably,tried to be honest :And candid in expressing my opinions, alwae-s knowing that it is only the wise man wko changes Ilia opiniuns, the other fellow, of course, never does. I also,always try to be as personal as possible when deal- ing with my own change in thought,and the" Examiner" file would show this fully with respect to two questions: those of socialism and of drink. Men of thought, take positives, or tentative views of all reform questions,and, if I know my own character, I think it has a fair amount of the positive in it, I also think thaS Mr. flow- croft found this to be so during the wire-drawers strikes some years ago, and he may acknowledge the results as being beneficial as distinguished from any possible re- sults that might have come about from his own eff arts, And views. Independent of personal characteristics, we also learn something from experience. Mr. Howcroft is, to day, a teetotaler in consequence of a verf,bitter lesson learnt in his youthful days. I am an abstainer because I have come to the conclusion that drink keeps labour in sub- jection to capital. Some dozen years ago I did pose as a "pot house demonstrator of temperance." Will Mr. Howcroft be surprised to know that I have not moved from that position one jot ? Were lin Warrington now I should go to the " pot house " whenever I thought fit, seeking, as of yore,to lift it, by example and speech to a higher grade. If Mr. Howcroft desires infertnetion on this point I refer him to my old friend Mr. Gleave of the Barley Mow. Mr. Howcroft says "To me temperance means teetotalism." Weil, I have no objection to him attaching any meaning he likes to the word. I think if I were to advise him to be temperate in sprech,ee would think I were insulting him, if he thousslt I wanted him to shut his mouth altogether, or if I urged him to temperate in eating I therefore wanted to rid the eat th of his presence. The above is all that concerns Mr. How- croft, and he need nut trouble to read the following few lines. I have always held " views " touching the teetotal question. I remember that twenty-five years ago and -when working at Whitecross I refused ti sit in a public house, or to take beer in atsy fashion with a youth. 'There may be congenial reasons for drunkenness. There may be a natural weakness in the nature of man, or there may be the force of examples leading on lo habit. Eut above and far away from these possible rEasens

===== 0002601_18940512_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
there certainly is the intolerable wear and tear of our physical and mental natures, or as it may be put our 19th century civilization to be held accountable for the present degrading supremacy drink has over man. There are various ways of meeting the difficulty. Local Option is not one. I often smile when I read in English papers of the good results et the Maine liquor laws. Laws against smuggling did not and does not prevent smug- gling, but laws removing the incentives to it might, and do where carried out. In my travels to and fro in this land I have several times been in cities where neither saloons nor any signs of drink could be seen. I was in Concord a few weeks ago. This is a model town. A few words with some workmen proved I could have as much as I liked. Every town in the States that votes prohibition just accomplishes this mush. It naakas the saloon keeper during the prohibition year &tibia his exertions to carry the open license vote for the following year. It also makes him exert himself to make up for lost time and profits. Worse than this, it encourages home drinking, familiarisiag the children with it in its most baneful aspects. Drink taken home leads the wife and children being invited te its refreshing influences. I had an example of this not two days ago. In a friend's house.Paaw...the child, just able to walk, go to the cage of beet a.nd'take a bottle out,and strugale with the cork. The mother carnet° its help, and poured out half a glass , which the child 'a t once drank. Most workmen think what is good for them is gr4od for wife and child. Tak- ing it home makes it convenient to carry out this wrong idea.

Again preoihition at once raises up a host of back street dealers who prosper immensely during such:a year. Having no license to pay and, as a rule, being shielded by the police, these dealers, will, each one of them, have as many customers as would carry on several open saloons. These people in the short time of one or two "dry " years make their fortunes, and with clew con- science, hope to have their reward hereafter. The or- dinary teetotaler and the broad cloth, and tall shiner cannot possibly know of these things. Beer goes out of a" wet " town into a" dry "town, in all the fashions that the genius and the inventive faculty can suggest. My conclusions are that Local Option does more to demoralize the moral nature of the ccmmunity than open saloons. I protest against the competition system, and would replace it by taking ex- perimental steps in Socialism, or in voluntary co-opera- tion. I judge by results, and protest against the methods of teetotal societies. I would, by law, take out of the trade all profits, and all politics. If a man ceases to profit, he will cease to sell. If there are no politics in it there will be no log-rolling. I would raise the stan- dard of the public house, not the American saloon, by taking my wife and family to its cosy, comfortable and respectable rooms, when we went " up street "or, say, when we went to Liverpool or Manchester. By bring- ing the full force of temperate public opinion ta bear right on the plague spot, I would shame the drunkard into temperance. I would, under present conditions, prohibit, by law, all societies, relating to trade or benefit, from meeting in or near public heuses. All towns should be made to provide, out of the rates, halls free for such purposes. Crime, as a result out of drink, cuts a small figure in beer-drinking countries. As a pre- liminary step I would have 1l spirits sold subject to the strictest analysis. Breaking this law should be followed by not less than six months hard labour, no fine. would also have a full knowledgeof the chemical features of alcohol made a part of every child's education. In conclusion I say I am only a teetotaler for example's sake.—Yours, &c.,

H. WFIITHAM.

A FALL DOWN A Har.p..—An accident of a serious ' character on Saturday morning befell Jr.l Joseph Light- foot, captain of the new flat Dog Star. it seems the Dog Star was lying in the docks at Liverpool, and was in the act of being drawn near the dock side by Captain Lightfoot and his mate, when suddenly die rope (which had been thrown to an attendant on shore to be put on the stumps) slipped, causing the captain to fall back- wards into the flat's hold, a distance of 13 feet. He fell on to his left side across the kelson. causing severe in- ternal injuries. .He was at once taken t) the hospital, where his injuries were examined, and in the afternoon he was assisted to his home in Penketh by his brother and Captain John Holden, where he now lies in a criti- cal condition.

Mr. Fred Dennett, secretary of the St. Helens Foot- ball Club, states that as a result of his attendance at the meeting of Lancashire championship secretaries on Mon- day night, fixtures between St. Helens and the other nine first division clubs were arranged for next season. The competition starts at the close of Septembw, and concludes on the 6th of April. SUSPECTED STABBING CASE.

A serious development has occurred at St. Ilelens in connection with the supposed accidental death of a labourer tamed Thomas Finn, lodging at 9, Edward-st. Wounds have been discovered in the deceased's neck pointing to his having been stabbed, and the inquest was adjourned on Thursday to permit a psi mortem ex- amination being made.

BOSPITAL SATURDAY.

The committee of the Free Providence Hospital, St. Helens, have decided, in order to raise a deficit of £4OO incurred last year, and to carry on the work, to have a Hospital Saturday on the lines of Liverpool and other towns. Councillor J. B. Leach has the arrangements in hand, and on Saturday next collecting cages will be placed in the princi?al thoroughfares. The committee earnestly appeal to the public to support an institution which does excellent service to the suffering poor and never closes its doors to an accident.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Alter a total of three hours and ten minutes fighting between the St. Mary's and Latchford teams, for the Warrington Junior Rugby Cup, presented for com- petition by Mr. Percy Davies, the St. Mary's team have at length won it. That they deserve it no one who saw them play on Tuesday evening will deny. Latchford began very strongly and at first appeared to be far and away the bettor team, but they were unable to keep this form up, and the St. Mary's team improved as the game went on, until they had very much of their own way towards the end, five tries to one being the score at t'ne close of the matcb. Latchford of course are to be con- gratulated upon the show they made, for they are evidently not so very far behind St. Mary's when it took so long to decide whe is the stronger.

There was of course a tremendous "gate" to witness the mat,th, which was net surprising when it could be seen fur nothing. The "close" season in football hay- ing arrived, any charge for admission to see the match is-illegal, and therefore the gates of the football field were opened wide for everyone who had any desire to see the match. The youthful element as usual under these circunastances was present in full force, and they speedily announced their presence by volubly expressing; their opinions of the play and players as the game pro- gressed. To come to the players, Burton and Flampson were unquestionably the pick of the winning team. Their collvagues in the three-quarter departmemt, Raybold and Warburton were also out of the common run. It was in this department whore St. Mary's excelled, as they were far superior tn the Latchford quartette. In the match of the preceding week Chapman the Latchford full back gave a better account of himself than Roughley for the Saims, but in the match on Tuesday. Chapman made numerous mistakes while Roughley played exceedingly well. In the half back and forward divisions, there was little to choose between the teams. In the forwards, Latchford at first were superior, but as the game went •on, there was little to choose between them. Burton maintained his consistency for scoring, for out of the five ,1 tries secured by Sc. Mary's he obtained three. Goal kick- ing was !.‘ off " very much, for although some of the tries were placed in pretty favourable positions, not one was increased to a goal.

Needless to say, the St. Mary's supporters were highly delighted at the success of their team. There was quite a sea of faces round the stand reserved for the commit- tee men of the Warrington club, to see the cup presented to Berry, the Sr. Mary's captain. The presentation was not mulch else than a formal affair however, for Mr. Davies seeing that his voice could not be heard above the Babel of tongues round about him did not attempt to make a speech, but contented himself with simply con- gratulating Mr. Berry upon the success o: his team. Mr: Ashcroft the president of the St. Mary's club, was however undaunted by the noise around him, and made a speech, and thanking Mr. Davies for his gift. His speech could scarcely be heard more than a yard or two away, but he was understood to say that the St. Mary's team intended to keep the cup now they had won it.

The cup competition has been without doubt a great success, and has unearthed a large amount of talent. Although only competed for by junior clubs and played in the evening, the attendances at the matches have been very large—a large as some of the first team matches. Should the competition become established, it will tend to keep up the interest in the Rugby code in sp:te of any counter attractions.

C RI CB FT.

Warrington journeyed to Werneth on Saturday to play the club of that name, and came back defeated. They have yet to win their first victory at Werneth, although whm they got the Werneth Men out for 73, victory seemed very possible, but the fates, and Hall, tke Wer- neth bowler decreed otherwise, and Warrington will have to wait another year before they have another chance of breaking the spell which hangs over them in this fixture, although they have the chance of revenge when the teams meet at Warrington. None of the Wer- neth team did anything exceptional with the bat, with the exception of Hall who" knocked up "41. The rest were nowhere. This was attributable in a great measure to the splendid bowling of Bates, the new pro- fessional for Warrington, who took six wickets for 25 runs, and had nine maiden overs out of a total of 19. Smith and G. Clarke also did well with the ball. Clark is usual displayed excellent wicket keeping, and the fielding of the Warrington team was very smart indeed, there not being a single extra scored.

The score of 73 was not a very tall one, but it was too muck for Warrington. The innings had not proceeded far before a rather unpleasant incident took place. Bates was given" run out." although almost everyone on this field was positive that the umpire was wrong in his decision. The rest of the t,am could do very little with Hall's bowling with the exception of R. Smith and Clark,. who compiled 19 and 20 respectively. The last three men scored the sum trital of none, so that War- rington were all dismissed for 53. Hall was responsible for this wholesale destruction, of his opponents' stumps, for he took eight wickets for 23. runs. The Warrington team was considerably vreaken,d at this match by the absence of Messrs S. M. and J. J. Crosfield, or otherwise they would probably have fared much better.

While the first team were suffering defeat at Werneth thbsecokid team were giving the quietus to the juniors from Werneth on the Arpley ground. I'Verneth com- piled 81 to which the II arrington team replied with 95, thus winning by 14 runs. Bibby and Bwn who took four wickuts for 20 runs, and three for eleven were the most successful bowlers on the Warrington side. Double figures were pretty numerous in the match, there being nine altogether. 22 was the highest figure on both sides Taylor being tl.ie scorer for Werneth, .and Duckworth for Warrington. The latter was also not out.

TEEE SHIP CANAL.

THE CANAL COMPANY'S NEW BILL,.

The Manchester Ship Canal Bill was oa Tuesday before the Select Committee of the House of Lords. • It sought sanction for the construction of several .tiales ; but the chief feature of the bill was • a special Clause ' under which the Company desired to be exeinptedlretn section 24 of the Railway and Canal Traffic'Act, This section set forth that the Company must Send to the Board of Trade a schedale of their tolls and c:D.,trges for revision. It was contended by counsel that the sec- tion should not apply to the Ship Canal Company; inas- much as they were only just making a business, and sufficient experience had net yet been obtained kith regard to the working of the tolls. The •Committee decided that the special clause asked for by the promo,. tors should stand part of the bill, but should' not apply to any property of the Company except the actual Mate- cheater Ship Canal. Drawing attention ta the wharlage rates payable to the Mersey Docks Board, Mr: Balfour Browne contended that as vessels proceeding directlY into the Ship Canal did not derive ally advantage from the piers and wharves of the Mersey DoCks Board, they should be freed from the obligation to pay wharfe rates upon goods which might be brought to Manchester by ship and afterwards returned to Liverpool. -The existing impost militated against ships coming upAlie canal. It was a great hindrance to trade, and the Side Canal Company asked to be relieved of it. The Com- mittee adjourned till to-day. The Manchester Ship Canal Bill was again before the• Select Committee of the House of Lords on Wednesday. The chief clause considered sought to exempt the canal docks sad basins from the dues of the Mersey 'DOeks lioar d for local or partial traffic. Mr. Marshall Stevens was examined, and said the Company did not object- to pay dues on cargo passing over the Mersey Docks, bu'V they thought it unfair that portions of cargo passing along the Mersey directly to the port of Manchester and then being returned to Liverpool should be liable te wharfage charges. After discussion, and with the sanc- tion of the Committee, the wharfage clause was adopted, to the effect that no vessel loading or discharging in ,the port of Manchester any part of its cargo which should be landed on any of the piers of the Mersey docks should, be liable to pay wharfage charges, but all vessels taking any such cargo from the port of Manchester to the piers belonging to the Mersey Docks Board or taking cargo therefrom to vessels in the port of Manchester should be subject to pay the Docks Board tonnage rates.

THE DEVELOPMENT OF THAITIC.

At the quarterly meeting of the Manchester Chamber . of Commerce, on Monday, the president, Sir Frank Forbes Adam, said it would be remembered by those who were present three months ago that a number of • members who were interested—and they were animated by pure and undiluted p,itriotism—interested in pushing on the work of the canal, thought it desirable to have a peblic meeting in Manchester, and to pass resolutions.-in the sense of furthering the interests of the canal. .They • consulted with the members of the chamber., and they thought that perhaps it would not lead to the practical results that were expected of it, and after taking coun- sel together and further considering the mater those interested in the movement abandoned it, o:rea.'-, any rate indefinitely postponed it, on the understanding that the board of the chamber would take measures and steps at an early day to appoint a special committee to deal with Ship Canal affairs, a committee that would be represen- tative not only of the merchants but also or the ship- owners and carriers and those who had cbarge- of the conduct of the affairs "f the canal. That completes hacl been formed, and he had no doubt it would be of great assistance in providing information and advice to thote who required counsel and in bringing together he most important shipowners on the one hand, and shippers- on the other. There was, he knew, a feeling, perhaps- a general feeling of disappointment at the slowness of the development of traffic on the canal, fie thought -that feeling was in some measure unreasonable-. (Hear, hear.) 'I hey could not expect a great undertaking like the Seip Canal to spring into sudden use.. Tho tract: must grow. It would do no good to strive or to cry.out. The trade would come, but it would comeein.,,its own way. They must remember h-w difficult it was to divert trade from its accustomed channels, teach,thr peeple of what had hitherto been an inland town te.set about the business of a port, and to competeewith the very rich and well-organized opposition both of the rail- way companies and of the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board. There was, he believed, sufficient vinor for th- canal, for the •r.tilways, and for Liverpool. I The canal would gradually find its proper place, becaus€s the canal route must in the long run prove the cheapest. If it was not the cheapest it ought never to have .been con- structed. But lie thought there could he no doubt that it woold prove the cheapest, and that it had a..-,,great and prosperous future before it. (Applause.)

GAS AINI WA'VEI3 COMMITTEE.

PI NANCr. COMM 1T32J4._

A meeting of this committee was held on the 2nd May. The borough accountant reported that " The varioils committees have furnished estimates of the expenses to be incurred in their several departments for the year ending March, 1895. uring the past year there -had been an excess of aetual,over estimated expenditure of £1,063. which absorbed, the whole of the estimated sur- plus of £B4l and left a,deficieney of £222. For the cur- rent year the estimated amount required was £12,107;t0 this must he added thadeficianoy of £222,making a total of £12,929. To raise this amount a rate of 25.2. in the £ would be necessory.' It was resolved that the Health Committee be reopested to give instructions whh a view to the orders from, the hospitals being better distributed amongst the local tradesmen. It was further resolved that a general district rata of 23. in the £ be levied for the year 1854-34, payable by two instalments,,the first on demand, and the other on the 30th September next.— Councillor Owens in moving the adoption of the minutes said if the various committees were going, to spend un to their estimates there would be a deflciency of £750 at the end of the year, and he appealed to them to keep their expenditure down,otherwise they would have their bills sent back by the Finance Committee, as they would have DO money to pay them with.—Councillor Barlow seconded,—After a long dilcussion9. and statements by the various chairmen of the committees as to keeping expenses down, the minutes were adopted.

THE HHA.UTH,OF. ST, HELENS. At the St. Helens Health Connii3tee on Wednesda.y,tibe medical officer reportsd.that during the past fortnight the death-rate was unusually low, being only 14.9, against 18.7 in the previous fortnight, and 21.5 in the corresponding period of last year. In the thirty-two weeks which had elapsed since September the deat:l-rate had only averagedl7•B: The deaths from zymOic dis- eases were 4, an the number of cases. of sicknpss was 46. Of scarlet lever there were 2 deaths and 28 new cases, and of typhoid fever 7 new cases. No,ether case of typhus fevr. had been reported in the Sutton district. As a result of a letter to the teachers of the various schwls regarding scarlet fever, three children had been found attending school in the most highly infectious condition by the teachers and promptly sent home. The number of patients in the Isolation Hospital on Saturs. day was twenty-five, The female sa:aitary inspectm had during April visited 981 houses in the poor distritts of Greenback, Gerard's Bridge, and rocket Nook. In 73 cases it was found that children were being kept in a dirty and unwholesome condition; 25 houses were lime-washed, and 43 persons had been served with notices for allowing houses t,a be in an insanitary cond!. tion. Dr. Robertson said he could testify to the fact. that the district of Greenback V 749 sh4Wing (lips, et a better condition.

HONOURS TO A WAR.RINGTON FIRM. The Longford Wire, Iron, and Steel Company, Limited, Warrington, have been awarded a first- which was opened by Prin- class certificate at the exhibition at pm.,ent being k. The South Eastern flail- cheesl sd ci nh sstlii ann) r di a, slit Kent, way have closely identified themselves with the exhibition, amongst the officials taking an active interest in it being Sir Myles Fenton (general manager). London; Mr. W. Wainwright (carriage and waggon superintendent), and Mr. Junes Stirling (loco. superir:- tendent), Ashford. The Longford Company's exhibits consist of (1) a London and North-Western set-ee (Wood's patent), as supplied to the railway company for waiting-rooms; (2) Wood's patent railway carriage seat of woven steel wire; (3) the same patent "applied to an ordinary chair; (4) Wood's patent bedstead, o it liwot,en steel wire mattress, galvanised ; (5) Wood's' 'Patent hospital bed ; and (6) Wood's patent invalid couch. As was the case in the Liverpool Exhibition of 1886, On the hospital bed mattress are placed forty weights of Hap., with the result that no injury or displacement whatever is caused to either the bed or mattress, whilst on a single wire a weight,of 4?-;t3wt. is suspended with an qually satisfact-,ry result. Since the Liverpool Exhibition rapid development has been made by the Longford Wire, Iron, and Steel Company, Limited, and the recent adaptations of their patent—viz., their railway carriage seats, are rapidly superseding the old-fazhioned coil spring, and have been adopted by most of the railways in the United Kingdom, whilst the principle is being introduced into omnibus seats, tramcars, &c., and ordinary chairs and , couches. The " Engineering "of last week gives a description and illustration of a form of railway carriage seats of which it states upwards of 13,000 have been supplied to various railway companies during the past few years by the Longford Wire Co. Limited, Warrington. It also adds that " The rapidity with which these seats have made their way is remarkable, when one remembers how conservative are railway companies, and how dis- inclined to admit alteration in standard patterns. Nearly every company in the kingdom has given trial orders, and most of them have repeated their orders on a large scale. In some cases the new seats have been adopted in a wholesale fashion ; the fUghland Railway has 1032 of them running, and the Great Eastern 7175. We can personally testify that these seats are most corn- fortable and though fastened from end to end, the movement of one passenger is not felt by his neighbour. Incleed so entirely localised is the deflection that it ts possible for a heavy man to dance a•break down at one end of the seat without disturbing anyone sitting at the other end. If railway passengers could only understand the sanitary difference between woven wire and tne boxes of stuffing that are usually employed there ,would be a general demand for the new seats."
